The Customer Paid. Then Silence: Five Updates a Small Business Forgets to Send

📅 Nov 20, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

The customer paid on Monday. By Friday, silence. The update lives in your head, not in a system — you meant to send it, you know exactly what it would have said, and the week filled up before you sent it. The customer is not angry yet. They are doing something worse: they are wondering. Wondering whether the money arrived, whether the work started, whether the date still holds, whether anyone is there at all. A paid customer who hears nothing starts to feel like a forgotten one, and a forgotten customer is one message away from becoming someone else's customer.

This article was drafted with AI assistance. It is written in plain English for people who run businesses, not for people who build software, and it makes no claim about any particular product. It is about the question this morning's post asked — what update is still waiting? — and it works through five updates a small business forgets to send, using the tools people already use, so you can check my working and tell me where I got it wrong. This is the twenty-seventh morning we have written about this gap, and the gap is still the same: the work gets done, and the telling does not.

A small business does not have a follow-up problem. It has five follow-up problems, in five places, and each one looks small on its own. The confirmation is only a sentence. The progress note is only a line. The delay warning is only a message. But a customer who gets none of the five hears nothing at all, and nothing at all sounds exactly like nobody cares — even when the work is going perfectly.

Here are the five updates, numbered, each one with the tools people already use. Each one starts with something ordinary. Each one ends with the same question: who was supposed to hear this?

The money arrived on Monday. The customer knows they paid; what they do not know is whether you know. A payment confirmation is the shortest message in business — we got it, thank you, here is what happens next — and it is the one most often skipped, because the person who should send it already knows the money arrived. The customer does not know that you know. For three days they carry a small doubt: did it go through, did it go to the right place, did anyone notice?

WhatsApp is where this message belongs, because WhatsApp is where the customer already is. A sentence in the same thread as the order, the same day as the payment, closes the loop before the doubt starts. A paper receipt does the same job for the customer standing in front of you — it is proof in their hand, and it costs ten seconds. The confirmation is not paperwork. It is the moment the customer stops wondering and starts trusting, and it takes less time than the wondering does.

The job is in progress. Someone is working on it, the parts are ordered, the first step is done. The customer knows none of this, because nobody told them. From their side, the silence after payment looks identical to the silence before anything happened. They paid on Monday, and by Wednesday they have no evidence that anything changed — so they send the message every owner dreads: "Just checking in — any news?"

That message is not a complaint. It is a customer doing your job for you, because the update never arrived. A short note on the day the work starts — we have started, here is the rough shape of the week, I will message again when the next step lands — turns a week of wondering into a week of waiting, and waiting is comfortable when someone said what is happening. Gmail does this well for the customers who live in email; WhatsApp does it better for the ones who live in chat. The tool matters less than the timing: the update has to arrive before the customer has to ask for it, or it is not an update at all.

This is the update that does the most damage when it is missing, and the one owners put off longest, because nobody enjoys sending bad news. The date moved. The part is late, the diary slipped, the supplier has not called back. The customer still has the old date in their head, and every day that passes without a new one makes the old date feel like a promise that was broken on purpose.

A delay notice sent early is not bad news. It is respect. "The date has moved to Thursday, here is why, here is what I am doing about it" turns a broken promise into a kept one — the customer was told, in time, by the person doing the work. A delay notice sent late, or never, turns the same delay into a discovery: the customer finds out on the day the job was supposed to happen, from the silence, and no explanation after that sounds like one. Send this one first, before the others, and send it the moment you know. The customer can forgive a moved date. They cannot forgive finding out last.

The work is finished. The order is packed, the repair is done, the document is ready to collect. The customer does not know, because the finished job is sitting in the same silence as the unfinished one. This is the strangest of the five, because the hard part is over and the only thing left is a sentence — and the sentence is the thing that gets forgotten, because the person who finished the job has already moved on to the next one.

"It's ready, here is how to collect it, here is what to check" is a message that ends the job properly. Without it, the customer waits for a call that never comes, and the finished work waits on a shelf for a customer who does not know to come. The notebook on the counter holds the jobs that are done; the message in the chat is the one that tells the customer. A finished job nobody is told about is a job that is not finished yet, in the only way that matters to the person who paid for it.

A week later, nobody has asked. The job was done, the money was paid, the customer went quiet — and the silence after a finished job reads, from the customer's side, exactly like the silence after a forgotten one. They do not know whether you remember them, whether you would notice if something went wrong, whether they are a customer or a transaction that closed.

The check-in is the cheapest message of the five, and the one with the longest memory. "How did it go? Anything I should fix?" takes one sentence, and it is the sentence that turns a one-time buyer into a returning one — not because of what it says, but because it proves someone was still thinking about them after the money was in. It is also the sentence that finds the small problem before it becomes the lost customer: the thing that was slightly wrong, the question that never got asked, the customer who was quietly unhappy and would never have said so unprompted. A business that checks in hears the truth while it is still small.

This is the kind of work an AI workforce is for — not the clever part, the ordinary part. The payment lands and the confirmation is drafted the same day. The work starts and the note is written before the customer has to ask. The date moves and the warning goes out the moment it is known, not the day the customer discovers it. The job finishes and the "it's ready" is ready to send. A week later, the check-in is drafted and waiting. Five silences become five messages. And nothing is sent before you have seen it. You read the draft, you fix a line if it needs fixing, you say yes — and the customer hears from you the same day, not the next week.

The relief is not that a machine wrote faster. It is that the telling stopped depending on a free minute at the end of a long day. You still decide what counts as ready, what counts as late, and what counts as worth saying. You still approve every word that leaves. The difference is that the drafting is done before you sit down, so the minutes you have are spent deciding — not remembering, at 11pm, the five messages that never went out.

The delay notice goes first, because it is the only one where silence actively does harm. A customer who is not told the date moved will find out on the day the job was supposed to happen, and that discovery costs more than any delay ever could. Tell them the moment you know, and the delay becomes a plan instead of a surprise.

The payment confirmation goes next, because it is the easiest to send and the quickest to earn trust — one sentence, the same day as the money, and the customer stops wondering. "We've started" follows, because it stops the "just checking in" message before it is sent. "It's ready" comes after, because a finished job that nobody is told about is not finished yet. The check-in goes last, not because it matters least, but because it only works once the other four have been sent: a business that never confirmed the payment and never warned about the delay has not earned the right to ask how it went.

That order is my answer. It is also a guess, and it is the part I most want corrected.

After a customer pays, five updates go missing: the payment confirmation, the "we've started" note, the delay warning, the "it's ready" message, and the check-in a week later. Each one is a sentence, each one lives in a tool people already use — WhatsApp, Gmail, a phone call, a paper receipt, a notebook — and the gap is between the job and the telling, and the owner is the glue. Let a workforce draft the five messages as the work happens, and keep the sending in your hands: you pick the order, you approve every word, and the customer stops wondering.

So — what update is still waiting? Which of these five is yours, and which would you send first? Tell me in the comments. I would rather be corrected than agreed with.

Ready-made AI agents for small businesses, built in public.

A Default-Deny NAT Firewall on OpenBSD pf, Managed with Terraform

📅 Nov 20, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Posted on Nov 20 • Originally published at blog.bsdserver.nl

pf is the best firewall I have worked with: one readable file, atomic reloads, NAT and queueing in the same language as the filter rules. What it lacks is an API. Every tool that wants to change a pf firewall ends up templating pf.conf, copying it over SSH and hoping pfctl -f likes it.

I wrote pfapid to fix that: a small REST API for pf that runs on the firewall itself, with a Terraform provider on top. This post builds a complete NAT firewall with it: a default-deny base, outbound NAT, a port forward to a DMZ web server, a blocklist and QoS on the uplink.

pfapid splits the firewall into two owners:

The base ruleset is the part you can't afford to get wrong, so the API can't touch it. Whatever Terraform does, the block and management rules in pf.conf come first.

The rules use interface groups (wan, lan, dmz, mgmt), never device names. When you replace a NIC or move a network to a VLAN, you add the new interface to the group and no rule changes:

pfapid ships as signed release archives for OpenBSD amd64 and arm64. Check the signature before you install it:

gpg must report a good signature from key 10A2F1058821D55C61E45C7EE97C02FEE2F51B23. Then on the firewall, as root:

The three empty files must exist before pf.conf includes them; pfapid fills them later.

pfapid has a built-in CA, so you don't need a PKI to get started. Create the CA and the server certificate, then a client certificate for Terraform:

Copy /root/tf-firewall.crt, /root/tf-firewall.key and the CA certificate (/etc/pfapid/pki/ca.crt) to the machine that runs Terraform, and delete the key from the firewall. A company CA, Vault or SPIFFE IDs work too; see CERTIFICATES.md.

This file is yours. It holds everything that must hold no matter what the API does:

Check the file, then load it with a way back. I locked myself out of my lab firewall while writing this post: my workstation wasn't in admin_nets. A background job that restores the old file after 90 seconds unless I cancel it brought the firewall back on its own:

The test has to be a new connection: existing SSH sessions keep their state and survive a rule change that would lock out the next login.

/etc/pfapid.json (mode 0600, root) lists the interface groups, tables and queue devices the API may use, and which client may do what:

The provider is signed with the same key as the pfapid releases; terraform init checks that for you.

Tables are lists of addresses that rules refer to as . Changing a table never reloads a rule, so it's the cheap way to add a web server or block a network. A table can hold IPv4 and IPv6 entries side by side:

Each pf_anchor is one anchor, api/, and its rules are loaded as a whole: pf is order-sensitive, so a ruleset is one atomic unit, never rule by rule. Split anchors along ownership: whoever owns the DMZ web server owns dmz-web.

The rdr-to rule changes the destination of connections to the wan address on port 80 or 443 to 10.0.20.10. The same pass rule creates the state, so there is no separate redirect-then-filter step to keep in sync. The redirected packet then leaves on dmz, and the web-out rules let it through: forget those and the port forward silently goes nowhere. Those same rules let LAN clients reach the web server directly.

For IPv6 the server has its own address, so there is nothing to translate: pass it in on wan and out on dmz.

quick ends evaluation on a match, so these blocks win over every pass rule in every anchor, whatever order pf evaluates the anchors in. log sends the dropped packets to pflog0.

Feed the table from wherever your threat data lives: a variable, a file, or a data source. Updating it is a table replace on the firewall, not a ruleset reload.

An upload that fills the uplink makes everything else on the line feel slow: VoIP breaks up, SSH lags, and downloads crawl because their ACKs queue behind the upload. HFSC queues fix that by sharing the uplink by rule:

Each anchor's plan includes pf_rules: the rules exactly as pf will load them, as pfctl -nv on the firewall printed them. This is what the plan showed in my lab (the QoS root was on vio1 there):

Lists become one line per port and per prefix, and pf adds its defaults (flags S/SA, round-robin). You see that before anything changes, not after.

Each change was loaded, confirmed by the provider over the same connection, and only then written to pfapid's state. A second terraform plan reports no changes.

Drift. I flushed an anchor by hand with pfctl -a api/dmz-web -F rules. The next plan:

The management interface. A rule that opens RDP on mgmt fails at plan time:

A rule pf won't take. pfapid runs pfctl -n during the plan, so a rule pf would refuse fails terraform plan, with pf's own error message, before anything is loaded.

Everything in this post was planned and applied with provider v0.1.0 and pfapid v0.1.2 on an OpenBSD 7.9 lab VM, and the pf output shown is what pf loaded there. The code, the design notes and the pf behaviour pfapid relies on are on GitHub: pfapid and terraform-provider-pf. Issues and pull requests are welcome.

Disclosure: I work on InterOdin, the API used in this tutorial. Any OpenAI-compatible provider plugs in the same way.

LangChain pipelines for security work, like log summarisers, alert enrichment or report drafting, break in an annoying way when the model refuses half the inputs. LangChain's OpenAI chat model accepts a custom base URL, so you can put Odin v1 behind an existing chain without touching the rest of it.

ChatAnthropic takes the origin, without /v1:

Token usage is reported in response_metadata on each reply.

Fewer refusals does not mean no rules: the Acceptable Use Policy forbids illegal and harmful uses, so keep it to systems and data you are authorised to work on.

The AI SDK's MCP drift check doesn't hash readOnlyHint: what that would miss, counted on 14,009 servers

📅 Nov 20, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Posted on Nov 20 • Originally published at modelmarket.dev

Since version 7.0.19 the Vercel AI SDK can fingerprint the tools of an MCP server. You call fingerprintTools when you first trust a server, store the result, and on later connects detectToolDrift tells you which tools were added, removed or changed. The package it ships in, ai, was downloaded 48.3 million times in the week of 12–18 November 2026.

The fingerprint covers three members of a tool definition: the description, the input schema and the title. Annotations are not among them. A tool that said readOnlyHint: true on the day you reviewed it can say readOnlyHint: false and destructiveHint: true later, and the drift check returns three empty lists.

We run HISTOR, a public record of what MCP servers advertise and when it changes, so we counted how often that happens. Between 23 October and 16 November 2026, 524 tools on 189 servers switched readOnlyHint from true to false. For 176 of those tools the fingerprint of the tool would not have moved. For 91 of them, on 32 servers, no tool on the server changed its fingerprint in that read, so the check would have had nothing to report.

The gap itself is not our find. NerdLevelTech showed it in September on a purpose-built server, for the output schema as well, and published a fingerprint over the raw tools/list that closes it. What a test server cannot say is how often this happens on servers people actually run. That is the part counted here.

This is read from the published source of ai 7.0.177 (src/generate-text/tool-fingerprint.ts) and @ai-sdk/mcp 2.0.101 (src/tool/mcp-client.ts). In our words, not the library's code:

Two things follow. Everything outside those three members is invisible: outputSchema, the hint annotations, _meta, icons, execution, vendor members. And the top-level additionalProperties of the input schema is replaced with false before hashing, so a server that changes it from false to true hashes the same.

The choice is defensible. The description and the schema are what the model reads, so they are where an injected instruction goes, and the function's own comment calls them the server-controlled, security-relevant fields. Hints are a different kind of risk: the model does not act on them, the host does. And hosts do. The same AI SDK documentation page shows an approval policy that lets a call run without asking when readOnlyHint is true, next to a note that annotations are untrusted hints. Cursor's Bugbot only uses MCP tools that a server marks read-only, according to Cursor staff. Where a host decides like that, the hint is part of what was approved. The release note is explicit about the division of labour: "Baseline storage and the drift response remain the app's responsibility."

A one-tool MCP server over stdio, read through the SDK's own client: once as reviewed, once after only the hints were edited, once after only the description was edited. The three files are server.mjs, pin.mjs and check_ai_sdk.mjs, with a package.json that pins ai 7.0.177, @ai-sdk/mcp 2.0.101 and zod 4.1.8. Node.js 22 or newer:

The first three lines are what the server sent and the fingerprint the SDK computed. With the hints reversed the fingerprint is the same string, and detectToolDrift has nothing to report. The description edit moves it. The last line is the same comparison over a hash of the whole definition, which is the fix further down.

HISTOR reads the tool list of every remote server in the official MCP registry without credentials, on a schedule, and of npm and PyPI server packages once per published version. Since 23 October it keeps every member of a definition, annotations included. The count below uses the snapshot of 16 November: every read between 23 October and 16 November in which something in a tool definition differed from the previous read of the same server.

The middle column is the per-tool view: the drift check may name another tool of that server, but not this one. The right column is the case where it names nothing.

These are not spread evenly. One PyPI package accounts for 37 of the 91 tools, and two hosts for 54 of the 230. They also stay: of the 176 readOnlyHint moves one had gone back by 16 November, and five of the 720 destructiveHint moves.

One case from the data: a remote server whose four tools all went from readOnlyHint: true, idempotentHint: true to false on both in one read, with nothing else changing. That reads like an author correcting annotations that were wrong. It is also exactly the edit that an approval based on the old hints would want to hear about.

Hints are a small part of it. Of 103,657 reads in which a definition changed, 6,338 would move no fingerprint at all: 6.1 % of reads, on 2,014 of 14,009 servers.

Most of these are _meta and outputSchema, and one host produces 2,805 of the 6,338 by itself. We also left out 174,484 reads in which the only thing that moved was a vendor's per-tool version member; counting them would have made the share look far larger and meant nothing. A hash over the whole definition moves on all of that, which is the cost of covering more: more alarms that turn out to be housekeeping.

FastMCP documents a fingerprint too (the "Tool fingerprinting" page, new in 3.0.0). Its base payload is the tool key and the input schema; the page says FastMCP does not define a single contract hash and leaves description, output schema, annotations and _meta for the author to add. It is meant for a server author checking their own tools in CI.

Used as it stands on the consuming side, it would not move in 10,742 reads where only tool descriptions changed, on 4,191 servers. A description edit is the plain form of tool poisoning, so anyone borrowing the recipe for a gateway should add the description first.

detectToolDrift is a plain comparison of two maps, so it works with any fingerprint; the September write-up takes the same route. The client's listTools() returns the definitions as the server sent them (its schema keeps members it does not know); hash those instead:

The last line of the run above is this pin on the hints edit. If the _meta churn of a server you depend on makes it too loud, drop that member before hashing and keep the annotations.

For servers configured in a project file (.mcp.json and the like), WARDEN keeps the same kind of pin in a lock file you commit. Checked on 19 November with the 0.31.0 package from npm and the one-tool server above:

That second command exited 0 while the server was unchanged and 1 after its readOnlyHint went from true to false.

Three files of the snapshot (161 MB), Python 3.9 or newer, no packages:

It prints the JSON the figures are drawn from (results). The file hashes are in the snapshot's signed manifest.json, and the README there says how a change is counted. A second script lists the 321 hint moves of the right-hand column one per line (CSV); it was written separately and arrives at the same 91 and 230.

We run HISTOR and build WARDEN. AI assistance was used for the count, the figures and this article. The data is the HISTOR snapshot of 16 November 2026 (CC BY 4.0); the commands above were run as printed on 19 November 2026.

Blocking GPTBot costs you 40% of your AI citations. The data is in.

📅 Nov 20, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

The most common GEO advice is "check your robots.txt." Most site owners assume they are fine because they never wrote a Disallow rule for GPTBot. The data says 25.8% of them are wrong.

I ran a pre-registered calibration study in October 2026. The goal was to test whether the GEO score predicts AI citations. It did not. But the study also collected a variable I did not expect to be this important: whether the site blocks at least one AI bot in robots.txt.

341 pages, sampled from the Tranco list, audited with GEO Optimizer 4.19.3. For each page, the tool reads robots.txt and checks whether any of the 27 tracked AI crawlers (GPTBot, ClaudeBot, PerplexityBot, OAI-SearchBot, and 23 more) are explicitly disallowed. The study also queried Perplexity Sonar with two templates per page and recorded whether the domain appeared in the source list.

The result was clear and not part of the pre-registered hypothesis:

That is an 18.6 percentage point gap. Sites that block AI bots get cited roughly 40% less often than sites that do not.

Most of these blocks are not deliberate decisions to keep content out of AI search. The audit data shows three common sources:

Legacy WordPress security plugins. Wordfence and Sucuri ship default rules that block unrecognized user agents. If you installed one of these in 2019 and forgot about it, GPTBot may be getting a 403 right now.

Inherited robots.txt files. Many sites copy a robots.txt from a template or a previous project. If that template was written before AI crawlers existed, it may not have Allow rules for GPTBot or OAI-SearchBot. The crawler falls through to a wildcard Disallow.

CDN bot protection. Cloudflare Bot Fight Mode, when enabled, blocks non-browser user agents at the edge. It does not touch robots.txt, so the block is invisible to any tool that only reads robots.txt. I found that 34.3% of Cloudflare-fronted sites in a 636-site sample block at least one AI crawler at the HTTP level, compared to 12.0% of non-Cloudflare sites.

In a fresh CDN-level scan of 513 reachable sites, the per-bot blocking rates are:

GPTBot is blocked more than any other AI crawler. 12 sites block only GPTBot and nothing else. That selectivity means someone read an article about OpenAI training data and added a Disallow, without realizing that GPTBot also powers ChatGPT's live web search. You can block training and allow citations by separating GPTBot (training) from OAI-SearchBot (search). Most people block both or neither.

14 sites block both GPTBot and PerplexityBot but allow Claude. That is a pattern: site owners who heard about the two most discussed crawlers and blocked them by name, without checking the full list.

If you have never checked whether AI crawlers can reach your site, this is the first thing to fix. Not llms.txt. Not schema. Not content structure. If the crawler gets a 403, every other optimization is invisible.

The fix is a robots.txt audit. Check which of the 27 AI crawlers are explicitly disallowed. If you use Cloudflare, check whether Bot Fight Mode or a WAF rule is intercepting non-browser user agents. If you use a security plugin, check its bot protection settings.

The calibration study is open. The raw data is in the geo-optimizer-skill repo. 341 rows, no domain names, shuffled order. You can reproduce the analysis.

If you want to check your site, run a free audit on geoready.dev. The audit tests all 27 AI crawlers at the robots.txt level and sends live HTTP requests as GPTBot, OAI-SearchBot, PerplexityBot, and Claude-SearchBot to check for CDN-level blocking.

The CLI is on GitHub, MIT licensed, 1,137 stars. pip install geo-optimizer && geo audit https://yoursite.com.

Circle and Trump-linked crypto firm hit with court freeze order after Binance’s $6.3 billion hack

📅 Nov 20, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

An Abu Dhabi court orders Circle and 17 firms to maintain freezes on crypto linked to Binance's theft ahead of a Dec. 1 hearing.

An Abu Dhabi court has ordered Circle and 17 other companies to keep tokens linked to Binance’s Oct. 29 theft frozen while the exchange tries to recover them.

Binance disclosed the order on Nov. 18 and said some issuers, exchanges and other partners ask for a court order before they will release or transfer assets they have frozen.

The exchange said the order from the Abu Dhabi Global Market Courts helps open that route, describing ongoing efforts to trace the stolen funds and giving no details of any transfer under the order.

The named firms include Circle, which issues USDC, and World Liberty Financial, the company behind USD1.

Reports noted that the firms must maintain the freezes pending further proceedings and cannot lend, swap, or mix the affected tokens with other assets.

The court did not accuse the firms of wrongdoing, and Dec. 1 is scheduled to consider whether to keep the injunction in place and on what terms.

The injunction covers specific assets Binance claimed are tied to the theft, and said it is still tracing the stolen crypto and taking legal steps to reclaim it. A transfer for firms seeking a court order depends on whether they have authority to release the specific balances they have frozen.

Tether falls outside the order covering the 18 firms, and the company said on Nov. 2 it had frozen more than $1.5 billion tied to the theft.

A spokesperson said Tether would work directly with Binance to reissue the tokens, in consultation with the relevant authorities.

The Abu Dhabi order follows earlier US court action. On Nov. 12, the US Justice Department said it had obtained court authority to seize or restrain more than $1.2 billion in stolen crypto. That was part of more than $2.7 billion that US authorities and private firms had helped freeze.

In its Nov. 3 incident report, Binance estimated that about $6.3 billion in digital assets were affected and about $2.85 billion was frozen or intercepted, subject to reconciliation and market prices.

The separate disclosures do not establish how much the figures overlap or how much Binance can ultimately recover. Binance says it absorbed the financial impact on customers while continuing to pursue the stolen assets.

Isaac Asimov’s Book Series Was Unfilmable — Now It's Apple TV's Greatest Space Opera

📅 Nov 20, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Sci-fi fans would collectively agree that some stories of the genre are better off staying on the page. For long, it was also believed that Isaac Asimov's books could not be translated to the screen, and for good reason. Especially when it comes to the Foundation book series, it was hard not to believe it was largely "unfilmable."

Some of the greatest sci-fi books are written in a way that it is easy to directly adapt them for the screen. Isaac Asimov's Foundation books, in contrast, defy almost every rule of visual drama. It unfolds less as a fictional drama and more as a deep dive into everything from sociological mechanics to human psychology. While at it, it also spans across centuries, shifting its focus from one character to another.

The books' massive time jumps, anti-cinematic action, and prioritization of ideas over character beats made it impossible to adapt for the screen. This explains why many planned big-budget adaptations of the books also struggled to see the light of day in the past. Fortunately, however, Apple TV's Foundation took a massive risk and achieved the impossible by successfully adapting Isaac Asimov's books.

Impossible feats demand extreme risks. Owing to this, Apple TV's take on the Foundation books significantly changed the source material's approach. The biggest adaptation challenge came from the books' revolving characters. Every few chapters, the books experience time jumps, where old characters die of age and new ones emerge.

On the screen, viewers need rootable visual anchors who appear for longer periods.

Apple TV's Foundation carefully navigated this issue by introducing cloning, cyro-sleep, and digital consciousness. These became narrative devices that allowed the series to logically sustain the same characters and cast members across centuries and seasons. Even an immortal humanoid, named Lady Demerzel, emerges in Foundation from Asimov's wider universe.

Unlike the books, Apple TV's Foundation also visualizes key conflicts and collapses from the original story instead of solely focusing on their political implications. Even "psychohistory" from Isaac Asimov's Foundation gets a visual and more tangible representation in the series.

Armed with the Socratic method, Apple TV's Foundation understands that "The beginning of wisdom is the definition of terms." Instead of treating Asimov's story as a plot to be faithfully transcribed, the show first understands the true essence of its terms. It then turns into a more sprawling space-opera by wisely redefining its ideas.

Many readers have complained that Apple TV's Foundation is too distinct compared to its source material. They argue that it is unfair to even call it a worthy adaptation. It is hard not to agree with them. An adaptation cannot be considered great if it is a far cry from its source material.

Similar to Stanley Kubrick’s The Shining and Denis Villeneuve's Arrival, Foundation fails as a book-to-screen adaptation on many levels.

However, if purely judged as a modern television space opera, Foundation is easily one of the best sci-fi shows of our time. While Asimov's books seemingly argue that historical forces matter far more than individual stories of heroism, Apple TV's Foundation carefully bends the original narrative to focus on almost mythic heroes and solo characters.

It is also arguable that a direct page-to-page TV adaptation of Isaac Asimov's Foundation books would likely have failed. Whether Apple TV's adaptation approach is ideal is still debatable, but it at least gives us an epic sci-fi space opera.

Isaac Asimov's novel series is brought to life in Foundation, a television adaptation of the epic sci-fi saga created for Apple TV+. The show follows a group of exiled humans that find themselves as the last chance to save the Galactic Empire, primarily from itself.

We want to hear from you. Share your perspective in the comments below, and please keep the conversation respectful.

L'attaccante francese, arrivato in estate dall'Atletico Madrid, deve così rimandare la conquista del suo primo titolo negli Stati Uniti.

China's Xi praises 'turnaround' in relationship with Australia

📅 Nov 20, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

The leaders of Australia and China have held talks on sidelines of APEC summit. Australian PM Anthony Albanese is also slated to meet Chinese Premier Li Qiang.

Australian Prime Minister Anthony Albanese and Chinese President Xi Jinping held talks on Friday, following which Xi hailed a "comprehensive turnaround" in the ties between two nations and Albanese said his country was engaging "constructively" with Beijing.

The leaders met on the sidelines of  the Asia-Pacific Economic Forum (APEC) summit in China's Shenzhen.

Xi pointed out to Albanese that they had met four times since the latter became Australia's Prime Minister in 2022.

"I think it is fair to say that together we have brought about a comprehensive ​turnaround in China-Australia relations," the Chinese President said.

Xi also called for enhancing cooperation in agriculture, ​energy, the ‌green economy and artificial intelligence, Chinese state news agency Xinhua reported.

China is Australia's top trading partner.

The relations between the two countries hit rock bottom under Canberra's former centre-right coalition government, which had called for an independent inquiry into the origins of the coronavirus.

An angry China retaliated by imposing import curbs worth $13 billion (€12.2 billion) on Australian timber, coal, barley, lobsters and wine.

Since Albanese's centre-left Labor party came to power, China has lifted most of its blocks on Australian exports.

But friction in ties has surfaced occasionally on issues such as China conducting a missile test in the South Pacific and Australia tightening a  foreign investment screening in the critical minerals sector.

"It is important that Australia continue to engage in a constructive way with China, manage our differences, but not be defined by them," Albanese told the media on Friday.

"What happens in China does matter for Australia," he added.

Albanese is expected to travel to Beijing and meet China's Premier Li Qiang later on Friday.

On Monday, Australia, which is the world's biggest producer of iron ore, called out China's "manipulation" of trade in the commodity over Beijing's formation of a central buying body to purchase ‌ore on behalf of its steelmakers.

A single buyer has increased bargaining power in negotiations with sellers.

Albanese and Li will attend a business roundtable comprising of senior executives from Australian mining giants Rio Tinto and BHP and ​Chinese company executives.

Charlie Austin reacts to 'strange' Swansea City manager development

📅 Nov 20, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Ex-Premier League and EFL striker Charlie Austin has branded the decision for Vitor Matos to leave Swansea City for Strasbourg as "strange," but does admit that the French club can be a "stepping stone" to bigger things.

The Portuguese boss's departure hasn't been formally announced by Swansea just yet, but Strasbourg themselves revealed that he'd joined their side to replace the outgoing Hugo Oliveira.

In the space of a little under a year, Matos took the South Wales side from 20th in the Championship to a top-half finish last season. Then, he took them to the league's summit during the opening stages of this campaign, before a run of four defeats in five saw them slip down to sixth.

Nevertheless, he had Swansea playing an incredibly attractive, high-intensity brand of football, while also being controlled in possession, too, and it was universally agreed that the Swans had their best chances of promotion in around six years under him.

Strasbourg currently sit 13th in Ligue 1 with three wins through their opening 10 games. They aren't in Europe, and some critics would say that the challenge of getting a team like Swansea back into the Premier League would be more satisfying than taking over a struggling team in France.

However, Strasbourg are under the BlueCo banner, the multi-club ownership group which mainly oversees Chelsea, and the French top-flight side is often seen as the little sister, and sometimes even a feeder club, to its Premier League siblings.

Speaking on his "Chaz Kicks Off" YouTube podcast show, former Burnley, QPR and Southampton striker Charlie Austin spoke about how now being under that umbrella could open doors for Matos that Swansea simply couldn't.

"The Swansea manager has departed and gone to Strasbourg, which is strange because they're in a good position," he said.

"But he's obviously been promised the world [by Strasbourg]; it's the stepping stone, isn't it? And we've all seen where it could lead. We saw it with that PE teacher who went to Chelsea, et cetera, so it opens up new things."

While a slightly disrespectful way to refer to him, the "PE teacher" that Austin referenced was Liam Rosenior, who was given the Chelsea job back in January following the departure of Enzo Maresca.

The 42-year-old had spent the previous season and a half at Strasbourg, leading them to a seventh-placed finish in his only full year at the club, and left them in seventh midway through the 2025/26 campaign.

At Chelsea, despite a promising start, seeing only Arsenal beat his side for the first two months of his time at the club, things quickly unravelled, and he was sacked in late April. He's now back in France, and has Paris FC sitting third in Ligue 1, three points off their neighbours PSG atop the table.

Like Matos, Rosenior was in a Championship job before moving to Strasbourg, and was shortlisted for Manager of the Season in his year at Hull City. The two are following a similar path, and the Portuguese boss may have taken the job with that potentially in mind.

What do you think Vitor Matos' ceiling as a manager is? Let us know in the comments.

In an ideal world for Chelsea, Xabi Alonso will be a massive success at Stamford Bridge, but if things start to unravel in a year or so, and Matos performs well at Strasbourg, he could be earmarked for that high-profile move. Arguably, as a Jürgen Klopp disciple, he'd have more of a chance than Rosenior did, too.

For Swansea, they now have to make sure that their position in the early-season promotion hunt wasn't down to Matos, and look to maintain the form that got them into the top six in the first place.

The Swans have a few big tests coming up to begin life post-Matos, with West Brom travelling to South Wales on Saturday, followed by a midweek trip to Middlesbrough and then the South Wales derby against Cardiff City a week on Sunday.

FLW gets AI to rank the friendliest clubs in the second tier this season

This decision from Vitor Matos to leave couldn't have come at a worse time, with not long left of the international break, and Swansea fans will be hoping that the disappointment surrounding it can help bring the squad closer together to pull off some big results.

Hopefully then, by the end of the month, Matos' successor will be in place, and they can help finish the good job that the Portuguese boss has started in the early stages of the campaign.

I ran 40 miles in the Saucony Endorphin Speed 6 — this smooth speedster handles every run

📅 Nov 20, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Keep exploring and earning more as a member.

Unlock and manage exclusive Tom’s Guide member rewards.

Join the Tom's Guide Club for quick access. Enter your email below and we'll send confirmation, and sign you up to our newsletter.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

The Saucony Endorphin Speed 6 maintains the fast, versatile ride the line is known for while adding a little more comfort, making it a successful if minor update on the Endorphin Speed 5. It’s one of the best fast trainers available and if you’ve loved past models, the Speed 6 is unlikely to disappoint, but there are some fantastic alternatives to consider too.

Why you can trust Tom's Guide Our writers and editors spend hours analyzing and reviewing products, services, and apps to help find what's best for you. Find out more about how we test, analyze, and rate.

The Saucony Endorphin Speed has been one of the most influential running shoes of recent years, setting the template for fast training sneakers that uses some of the materials and tech from top carbon plate racers.

With the line proving so successful, Saucony has been reluctant to change much about the shoe over the five generations to date, and even though the Endorphin Speed 6 is the biggest update yet thanks to a new midsole foam, it mostly retains the same ride feel as past models.

I’ve reviewed every model of the Endorphin Speed and I’ve always been a big fan. While that’s still true with the Speed 6, this category contains some of the best running shoes available, and there are some lighter, bouncier fast trainers I rate very highly, like the New Balance SC Rebel v1 and Dynafish Xiaonian.

The Saucony Endorphin Speed 6 costs $180 in the U.S. and £180 in the U.K., which is a $5 price rise on the Endorphin Speed 5. It’s an expensive shoe, though not out of line with other plated trainers like the Hoka Mach X3 and New Balance SC Rebel v1.

Right now the Saucony Endorphin Speed 5 is widely reduced, dropping to $104 on Amazon in the Black Friday sales, which is a factor to consider when weighing up buying the Speed

The Endorphin Speed 6 is currently available in five colors in the women’s shoe, and four colors in the men’s, including the white and yellow design I tested.

There is a wide version of the shoe available, though I found that the standard one fit me well in my normal running shoe size, the same size I’ve used with all past models of the shoe.

Get instant access to breaking news, the hottest reviews, great deals and helpful tips.

Saucony has added 2mm of foam to the midsole of the Endorphin Speed 6, which has a stack height of 38mm at the heel and 30mm at the forefoot, maintaining the 8mm drop of past models.

It weighs in at 8.5oz, which is 0.5oz lighter than the Endorphin Speed 5 in my size, a welcome drop for a speed-focused shoe.

The Endorphin Speed 6 has a simple mesh upper that has a little bit of padding around the tongue and collar, but mostly focuses on being lightweight and breathable.

It’s my preferred kind of upper on any shoe, since it’s not oppressively padded on the tongue or overly stiff around the heel, and I found the fit comfortable and secure throughout all my runs.

The Endorphin Speed 6’s main changes are in the midsole, which is made from a new foam called PWRRUN PBX, which is meant to be lighter and bouncier than the PWRRUN PB that has been used on all past models of the shoe.

There’s also the higher stack and a redesigned nylon plate in the midsole, which aims to create a smoother, faster ride, with the Speedroll rocker on the shoe also contributing to this.

The changes to the midsole are noticeable on the run, though not as dramatically as I expected when looking at the spec sheet for the Speed 6.

Rubber is used sparingly on the bottom of the Speed 6 to keep the weight down, but patches cover the forefoot and heel of the shoe, and in my experience Saucony’s pelletized midsole foams are durable even when the foam is exposed on the bottom of a shoe.

Grip hasn’t always been a positive feature on the Speed line, but the Speed 6 provided me with reliable traction in wet conditions on both the road and the track during my testing.

After the very pleasant surprise of the original Endorphin Speed, which I ran over 300 miles in, I’ve found that the line has delivered a reliably similar experience with all of the models since.

There have been updates to make it more stable and comfortable, but ultimately all five versions of the Speed to date have had a smooth, fast and versatile ride feel.

This is still true of the Endorphin Speed 6 for me, and while that’s mostly a positive in that it performed well across a variety of runs in my testing, some might have hoped for a bigger change to challenge rivals like the SC Rebel v1 and cheaper, lighter options like the Adidas Adizero Evo SL and Dynafish Xiaonian.

I used the shoe for a couple of easy runs, two progression runs moving from easy to tempo pace, and a threshold track workout running 4 x 8min reps, and it felt great throughout.

I used the shoe for a couple of easy runs, two progression runs moving from easy to tempo pace, and a threshold track workout running 4 x 8min reps, and it felt great throughout.

The rockered midsole pushes you smoothly through your footstrike, and while the midsole foam isn’t as soft and springy as some, it does give a good amount of energy return off the toes.

I also found the Speed 6 to be quite stable and comfortable for easy runs, which is not always the case with plated trainers like this. I could happily use it for pretty much any kind of run, though it’s not as fast as a true carbon plate racer, or as comfortable as a max-cushioned cruiser.

To compare it directly to the Endorphin Speed 5 I did a short run wearing both shoes, and while the Speed 6 is a little bouncier and more comfortable, especially under the forefoot, I still felt the two shoes had a similar ride feel.

The Speed 6 is still a bit firmer than most super-trainers, and not as comfortable as more cushioned shoes under the toes. Some will like this more direct ride feel, but I appreciate the extra softness and bounce of a shoe like the SC Rebel v1.

If you’re a long term fan of the Endorphin Speed line the Speed 6 will be a winner. It’s a mild improvement on the previous models with extra cushion and bounce, and delivers the versatile performance you expect from the line.

There is certainly a case for getting the Speed 5 instead in sales in my opinion, but the Speed 6 is a slightly better shoe overall and a successful update.

The Speed line has been faced with increasingly fierce competition in recent years, and I don’t think the Speed 6 does much to change the equation on this front.

If you want a lighter, bouncier fast trainer, the New Balance SC Rebel v1 would be my pick, while plate-free shoes like the Adidas Evo SL, Dyanfish Xiaonian and the new 361° Mega 3 Pro all offer fast, fun rides at a lower price.

As someone who would always have had the Endorphin Speed in my own rotation through the first few models, I’ve now moved on to shoes like this myself. Even though the Speed 6 is just as good as past models, I personally like the springier ride available in fast trainers.

Nick Harris-Fry is an experienced health and fitness journalist, writing professionally since 2012. He spent nine years working on the Coach magazine and website before moving to the fitness team at Tom’s Guide in 2024. Nick is a keen runner and also the founder of YouTube channel The Run Testers, which specialises in reviewing running shoes, watches, headphones and other gear.

Nick ran his first marathon in 2016 and became obsessed with the sport. He now has PBs of 2hr 25min for the marathon and 15min 30sec for 5K. Nick is also a qualified Run Leader in the UK.

Nick is an established expert in the fitness area and along with writing for many publications, including Live Science, Expert Reviews, Wareable, Coach and Get Sweat Go, he has been quoted on The Guardian and The Independent.

The Premier League’s newest managers have a lot to lose

📅 2026-11-20  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Farewell then, Michael Carrick and Pierre Sage, who have gone off to the great big Premier League managerial scrapheap in the sky after an international break where only the identity of the next James Bond could rival speculation over Carrick’s eventual replacement in particular.

In Mauricio Pochettino and Thomas Frank respectively, Manchester United and Crystal Palace have gone for a pair of managers whose reputations in English football could do with a good start this weekend to avoid the varying levels of doom that have surrounded Old Trafford and Selhurst Park from peaking again.

It’s a good thing, then, that despite their contrasting styles, both are known for putting emphasis on energy – not just in how their team plays on the pitch, but in how the culture around the club feels off it.

Pochettino has made headlines before for his love of lemons (they absorb negative energy, he’ll have you know) and his apparent sixth sense. In his 2017 book Brave New World, he wrote: ‘What most influences my decisions is my ability to see if the right energy is flowing.’

What kind of energy he felt flowing through director of football Jason Wilcox is anyone’s guess at this stage, but his emphasis on things like togetherness and the mindset of his players could finally be the key to starting to turn the Suez canal tanker that United have become, on the pitch at least.

Read exclusive analysis of the biggest stories in football with Metro's weekly football newsletter In The Mixer. Sign up now for free.

To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video

If Pochettino comes across as part high-level manager, part yoga teacher, then Frank has the air of an executive ready to rebuild the culture at an ailing business.

A year off from football was on the cards after he was sacked by Tottenham in February. The Dane turned down Palace during the summer in favour of World Cup punditry and attending the Tour de France, and didn’t sound completely convinced he’d made the right decision to cut that break short.

‘I was a little bit, “When do I want to go back in? Is it now?” Then this opportunity came up,’ he told PalaceTV this week. ‘I was thinking, this is probably too good to turn down. When I’m sitting here, walking around the training ground, it was just, “Okay, you’re back”, which scared me a little bit, but it’s good.’ Consider the troops rallied.

Questions surround the suitability of both appointments, as Pochettino’s biggest success at Spurs was built on the back of a young, mobile team with something to prove and Frank’s at Brentford came as part of a well-oiled machine. You’d be waiting a long time for anyone to describe United or Palace as either of those these days.

Use AI to go deeper into the stories you care about – powered by Metro and trusted publications.

There’s hope to be found too, for those willing to look for it. Frank did only really have half a bad season and the perceived wisdom is that his personality will suit a ‘smaller’ club – as long as he stays away from any Brighton-branded coffee cups – while of all the managers tasked with fixing United, Pochettino does have the air of a man who can become a messianic figure.

Managerial reputations can only survive a certain number of kicks before there’s no coming back and there may not be many chances left for either man at this level if things go pear-shaped.

However, with both teams playing pretty much every three days for the next month, there are plenty of opportunities for their new managers to shift the vibes back towards a positive setting.

And even if the wins don’t start flowing, transfer enthusiasts should have something to cling on to as Palace and United are reportedly ready to back their new men with some signings once the January window opens.

It’s nice to have something to look forward to after Christmas, at least.

Worse Than 2008? Peter Schiff Issues Terrifying Market Crash Warning

📅 2026-11-20  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Noted economist and longtime Bitcoin critic Peter Schiff has sounded the alarm about an upcoming financial apocalypse, warning that the crisis could be worse than the historic 2008 market crash.

On Nov. 20, Peter Schiff shared a series of troubling X posts warning about the potential for significant losses in the U.S. stock market, real estate, and cryptocurrencies amid escalating financial system stress.

The economist shared a video with a very alarming headline: "The Next Crash Will Be Worse Than 2008."

In one of his most recent posts on X, Schiff argued that the recent gold price downtrend might signal a grim outlook for other key asset classes.

"It's probably a bad sign that gold is falling this much. It probably means that the stock and real estate markets are going to fall even more in percentage terms," Schiff cautioned.

According to Peter Schiff, gold's recent price behavior could be signaling deflation. As a result, this might lead to a more severe financial crisis.

However, Peter Schiff is convinced that the Federal Reserve will step in to prevent this financial apocalypse.

"Gold is signaling deflation. But asset deflation would bust the government and the banking system, so the Fed will pivot to avoid it," the economist explained.

Simply put, Schiff assumes that the Federal Reserve will pivot and introduce monetary stimulus to prevent a financial meltdown.

In another startling prediction, the economist warned that the U.S. stock market might wipe out all its gains since President Donald Trump took office.

"There's a good chance that the U.S. stock market is about to give back all the gains since Trump took office," the economist predicted.

He also warned that there might be a recession. As a result, Trump will likely point to Democrats and their wins in the recent midterm elections as the reason for this recession.

Interestingly enough, Peter Schiff's bearish outlook also applies to Bitcoin (BTC), which has already suffered significant losses amid the overall market crash.

On Nov. 18, Peter Schiff shared several alarming market indicators. According to his data, the Nasdaq has dropped 12.25%. AI infrastructure company CoreWeave has plummeted 64%.

Bitcoin, on the other hand, has plunged 33.5% from its 2026 peak.

Additionally, the economist noted that the U.S. housing market index had dropped to 25, its lowest level since 2012. This shows that the economic issues go well beyond AI stocks.

"Still think this is just about AI?" he asked.

UK and EU to agree youth mobility scheme, BBC understands

📅 Nov 20, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Watch: Burnham says he wants 'best deal for Britain' at UK-EU summit

Up to 100,000 young people from the EU would be allowed to travel, work and study in the UK per year under a new youth mobility scheme set to be agreed on Friday, the BBC understands.

The same number of young Britons would be allowed into Europe under the scheme, expected to be approved at the UK-EU summit in Brussels. Downing Street has not commented on the details, but said talks were "down to the wire" on Thursday.

The prime minister has said a "Channel action plan" on immigration could also be struck, while negotiators are seeking agreements on food trade rules and carbon emissions pricing.

Conservative leader Kemi Badenoch has accused Andy Burnham of "reopening the divisions" of the Brexit referendum.

A new youth mobility scheme would aim to make it easier for young people to work and travel both in the EU and UK.

The UK already has schemes in place with 13 non-EU countries including Australia, Canada and India.

In 2024, the EU proposed allowing 18 to 30 year-olds to work or study in the EU and UK for up to four years.

Labour, which was then in opposition, rejected the deal - arguing it amounted to reinstating freedom of movement.

Since coming to government, the party has suggested it would consider a more restricted scheme with a time limit, a cap on the numbers coming to the UK and a ban on participants from claiming benefits.

The UK's request to limit numbers has proved to be a sticking point in negotiation ahead of the summit, along with the EU's request that EU students should pay the same tuition fees at UK universities as domestic students.

Speaking to broadcasters on Thursday evening, Burnham was asked if there were any circumstances in which the UK would walk away from a deal on Friday.

"There would always be those circumstances because I'm in this to get the best deal for Britain, always," he said.

"But what's in it for us is the possibility of lower food prices and I can't turn my back on that, lower costs for business, cutting red tape, and a really big win potentially of more collaboration on immigration - a Channel action plan - which is a really big deal if we can land that tomorrow.

"So I hope people can understand why I have pushed this because it could be a really good deal for Britain."

Burnham previously said the UK and EU could link up their law enforcement efforts, including sharing more data and criminal records of foreign nationals.

The prime minister's spokesman has said if a deal were secured, the government would introduce legislation to Parliament next week to implement changes.

The Brexit debate is back â and there are nerves about what happens next

Faisal Islam: What's in the Brexit reset food deal and are there trade-offs?

The EU that Burnham is eyeing up is very different to the one Britain left

An agreement on trade could remove checks and paperwork faced by food companies exporting to the EU.

The government argues this could add Â£5bn to the economy and cut food prices.

However, it could mean the UK will have to follow certain EU rules on products, for example on gene-edited crops or labelling.

Burnham has also said the summit would allow the UK to raise concerns about the Made in Europe scheme, which is designed to protect industries from unfair Chinese competition.

Burnham has previously suggested he could commit Labour to building a closer long-term future relationship with Europe, including asking to rejoin the organisation as a full member - however, he has ruled out major changes before the next election.

The party's 2024 manifesto promised not to return to the EU, the single market, customs union or freedom of movement, which allows EU citizens to live and work elsewhere in the bloc without a visa.

Earlier this week, former deputy prime minister David Lammy urged the prime minister to go into the next general election promising to rejoin the EU.

However, the subject has divided his cabinet with one minister warning of the costs that would come with reversing Brexit.

Reform UK leader Nigel Farage, speaking to The Sun, external, said he does "not believe for a minute" the country would "ever vote to rejoin" the EU.

He added: "I think what Burnham is doing is playing a very cynical political game. He's trying to pick up votes from the Lib Dems, pick up votes from the Greens, and using it as a massive distraction from all the problems that he can't deal with."

Speaking to the BBC's Today programme on Thursday, Badenoch said she wanted to see easier trading with the EU, but "not trade at any cost".

She also said the new deals could see UK companies having to follow EU rules, even those that do not trade with the bloc.

Appearing on BBC Breakfast on Friday, Reform UK leader Nigel Farage described the agreement as a "very, very expensive and costly error" and said Burnham has no "democratic mandate to do any of this".

He added that the Labour party manifesto was clear that the UK would not go back towards the single market and asked: "Where is our democracy?".

Liberal Democrat leader Sir Ed Davey told BBC Newsnight that while he agreed with Burnham on "the harms of Brexit", he did not think the prime minister was "going to go anywhere near far enough".

"We need to go further and faster," he said, including returning to freedom of movement - which Sir Ed said would help tackle immigration and boost the UK economy.

Mario Frick savours Aberdeen ‘paradise’ as heavy metal football takes precedence over cynical rave murmurings

📅 Nov 20, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Although he rates it as one of his best football memories, Mario Frick learned a painful lesson after scoring against Scotland at Hampden 16 years ago.

He injured himself in the resulting melee with his Liechtenstein teammates and had to be substituted. “They gave me an injection but it didn't help,” he recalled. "In the seventh minute of stoppage time we lost 2-1 - which is football. It was my last year in international football, maybe my last goal for the international team at around 36-years-old.”

Did you know with a Digital Subscription to The Scotsman, you can get unlimited access to the website including our premium content, as well as benefiting from fewer ads, loyalty rewards and much more.

It was a reminder of the perils of over celebrating. The cynic might say the new Aberdeen manager has come to the right place. The Pittodrie side haven’t scored for three matches and have recorded the fewest shots on target in the league this season.

There doesn’t seem much danger of him erupting with joy and bumping his head on the roof of those tight Pittodrie dugouts. Albeit, he will have to wait for the chance to take a bow at the stadium having replaced the sacked Stephen Robinson during the international window. Trips to Rugby Park and then Ibrox await before he can enjoy the comforts of home for the first time against Dundee at the end of this month.

Although there was no game on at Pittodrie on Thursday, Frick's face adorned the jumbo screen in the corner as he was officially welcomed to the club ahead of being “formally unveiled”, as the club described it. Even the empty stadium, he said, gives him “goosebumps”. He regards it as a temple. Nowhere is the reverential air more intoxicating than in the Sir Alex Ferguson Lounge, where Frick was presented to reporters for the first time. “Pure football,” he said, while gazing at the photographs adorning the walls.

The Aberdeen training facility at Cormack Park is better than anything he encountered in Switzerland, where he played and managed. “It is paradise,” he said. He’s clearly keen to linger awhile, to which the reply might be: Good luck with that. While he occupies a unique position as the first Liechtenstein player to become a professional footballer, Frick is slightly less exceptional when it comes to Aberdeen.

The 52-year-old is the 27th permanent manager at Pittodrie, stretching back to 1903. That averages out at about one every four-and-a-half years, which isn’t too bad. However, as many as 16 have come and gone since Ferguson left for Old Trafford 40 years ago this month. The recent anniversary celebrations – Manchester United being the ones celebrating rather than Aberdeen, who have rued the day almost ever since – were a reminder of the significance of that November’s morning in 1986, when it was announced he was heading south.

No club is immune to the difficulties of trying to replace a successful manager, as Manchester United have spent the last 13 years proving. Aberdeen have not been completely feckless without Fergie. A Scottish Cup here, a League Cup there. They haven’t been relegated either. Not yet at least.

Steering the side away from danger is Frick’s first task, starting this weekend against the only team currently below them: Kilmarnock. He knows what’s involved having taken over at his previous club, Luzern, five years ago when they were deep in relegation trouble. They parted company this summer.

“They were bottom of the table with 18 matches and 11 points when I took over there and the whole environment was similar to now,” he said. “We saved them and progressed from there. I am sure I can do the same now at Aberdeen. I am very ambitious and now it is important to get out of this situation and we want to go to the top six.”

The noises coming out of Pittodrie in recent days is that Frick likes to play ‘front foot, attacking football’, which seems to mirror the promises made by every new manager these days. Isn’t that what Sebastien Pocognoli trumpeted when he was unveiled as Scotland manager three months ago? The alarm bells might be growing louder for Aberdeen fans. Frick explained his “heavy metal football” philosophy. “It is high pressing, it is counter pressing, it is vertical play and creating many goalscoring chances,” he said. “That is heavy metal.”

Supporters will argue that anything’s better than what a team coached by Frick’s predecessor was producing. Aberdeen chief executive Adrian Bevington referred to the new manager's “warm personality” and suggested local journalists should look forward to getting to know the new man. Frick is an urbane character though one it might be advisable to avoid crossing. Trim and sporting a military haircut, he doesn’t look as if he will accept having his authority challenged.

As for the vexed question of the captaincy, with Kevin Nisbet giving the impression that the responsibility weighs heavily on him, Frick confirmed the striker will retain the armband against Kilmarnock. “Afterwards, I will have to decide what we do,” he said.

Frick speaks five languages with various levels of proficiency. His German and Italian are perfect. “English I need to improve,” he said. “I started learning English in school but never used it.” He recently started English tuition online “with an Australian who lives in France” with a view to working at a British club. “My only ambition now is to bring success to Aberdeen,” he said.

The fans will hope that such a zeal for self-improvement is catching. When Ferguson once ordered his players to have no fears about going to Glasgow and winning, now a sense of trepidation infects so much at the club. Aberdeen have just announced that a “Hacienda Classical” rave night event at Pittodrie on 28 May will no longer be going ahead. Someone queried online whether it was a case of the calendar needing to be left clear for relegation play-offs. Confidence is rock bottom.

Frick relishes the challenge. “I am ready!” he said. “I have had a five-month holiday! I am full of energy.”

This 16-Year-Old Founded an AI Missile Startup. He’s Targeting ‘Cost per Kill’

📅 Nov 20, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

At 16 years old, Henrijs Dandzbergs is too young to vote. Or drink. Or smoke. Or drive unsupervised. But he’s not too young to win half a million dollars for his AI-guided-missile startup.

In September, Dandzbergs, who was born in Latvia, was admitted to Y Combinator’s fall 2026 cohort. The startup accelerator counts companies like Airbnb, Stripe, DoorDash, and Reddit among its alumni; Dandzbergs is likely the youngest solo founder in its vaunted history.

His startup, Polemos Industries, is attempting to develop a new class of precision-guided missile that can be easily and cheaply manufactured, can be fired from portable launchers, and can resist jamming attempts. “I think missiles are the coolest thing,” Dandzbergs tells WIRED. “A lot of people that have found success in engineering early on have had a passion for blowing stuff up.”

In conversation, Dandzbergs is poised and articulate. He talks about “attacking hard problems,” breaking things down to “first principles,” and how the Ukraine conflict has forever altered the theater of war—a personal fixation, given his country's proximity to Russia. He projects an unassailable confidence in his own abilities.

“He’s a pretty exceptional person,” says Harj Taggar, a YC partner assigned to mentor Dandzbergs. “I’m still somewhat in disbelief that we’re talking about a 16-year-old.”

Dandzbergs has resolved to apply these unusual talents to figuring out how to optimize targeted killing. His startup’s guiding ambition, according to its investor one-pager, is to bring down the “cost per kill.”

As a young(er) kid, Dandzbergs was rarely in one place for long; his family moved between Latvia, Indonesia, Australia, and Spain. His surfer father, part owner of an insurance company, likes to go wherever he might find the raddest waves.

Early in his schooling, which mostly took place online, Dandzbergs demonstrated a clear talent for science and math. His room started to look like a lab, littered with schematics, robotics, Raspberry Pis, and 3D-printed components.

When Covid struck, Dandzbergs’ family returned to Latvia. His parents enrolled him in Astra Nova, an Elon Musk–linked school where students undergo an experimental curriculum built around lateral thinking and problem-solving instead of rote memorization. Dandzbergs took a class taught by a NASA employee and became fascinated by aerospace engineering. His lab creations became ever more elaborate: a bionic arm, a thermal control system for a model rocket, sensors for a self-flying drone.

Around this time, Russia invaded Ukraine. In Latvia, Dandzbergs started to see military jets overhead, and stray missiles were intercepted at the Russian border. With the sovereignty of Eastern European nations under threat, new defense startups began to pop up across the region. Dandzbergs wanted in.

“A patriotic intention to protect my country, European countries, and Ukrainian and American soldiers, and understanding the future of autonomous warfare—that’s my drive,” he says.

To stretch his technical skills, Dandzbergs started to take part in hackathons, where software and hardware engineers compete to develop the most sophisticated prototype from scratch.

In April of this year, he flew to London to take part in a hackathon held by Unicorn Mafia, an invitation-only group of developers. The prize included $10,000 in cash, a plane ticket to San Francisco, and free legal assistance with O-1 visa applications to stay in the US.

Less than 48 hours later, Dandzbergs and his two teammates, Aleksandrs Tihenko and Kai Yang, had built an AI system that could design hardware circuit boards from natural language prompts, much like how a software engineer might use AI to generate code. Though his teammates are both a few years older, Dandzbergs pushed for them to include even more intricate features in the prototype. “It was amazing,” says Tihenko. “The imagination of Henrijs in the hardware space is just crazy.”

Seeing that they stood no chance of beating Dandzbergs’ group and other front-runners, teams started to drop out of the competition, according to Fergus McKenzie-Wilson, Unicorn Mafia’s director and one of the hackathon judges. “What they built was incredibly impressive,” he says. “No one had ever seen that before.”

When the organizers crowned Dandzbergs’ team victorious, somebody handed him the mic. “I’m 16 years old,” he said.

The week after the hackathon, Dandzbergs and his teammates pitched their circuit board concept to YC. They won a place in the summer program, but Dandzbergs wavered over whether he should drop out of high school. The trio also sparred over whether the startup should focus on applying the technology to the defense sector, Dandzbergs’ primary area of interest. Dandzbergs ended up pulling out of the partnership. “It was hard,” says Tihenko. “But ultimately we just settled that the best decision for the company would be to part ways and focus on different things.”

By the summer, Danzbergs was set on building a defense startup and reapplied to YC on his own. He had spotted a gap in the market, he claimed, for AI-guided missiles that were more precise and could carry bigger warheads than the drones deployed widely in Ukraine. His products, he added, would be cheaper and easier to manufacture than hulking great rocket systems.

YC initially tried to steer him away. Dandzbergs was promising a lot of everything, all at once. It wasn’t clear whether his idea could work or whether pulling out of school was the right thing for him to do. “There’s a paternal element to it,” says Taggar. “You don’t want to be screwing up people's lives—and working on a startup is a pretty serious thing.”

Dandzbergs is up against a fleet of defense companies—from Anduril to CoAspire to Leidos—aiming to develop cheaper, mass-producible missiles. The more sophisticated the guidance system, the more difficult that task becomes.

“You can build a cheap missile that doesn’t go very far. You can build a cheap missile that doesn’t carry much of a payload. You can build a cheap missile that has a low reliability rate,” says Tom Karako, director of the Missile Defense Project and senior fellow at the Center for Strategic and International Studies. “There are trade-offs.”

But Dandzbergs wasn’t going to be dissuaded, and YC figured that somebody else would probably invest in his idea. The incubator invited him to join its fall program. “Big picture, you never know if these things are possible or not,” says Taggar. “Is it a non-zero-percent chance that it could be possible? That’s frankly the threshold, especially if it’s a really big [idea].”

So far, according to Dandzbergs, Polemos Industries has developed the software system that will guide its missiles and has begun to hammer out elements of the hardware design. By the time the YC program finishes in December, he hopes to be able to showcase a prototype missile.

If he does ultimately succeed, though, Dandzbergs will need to confront an assortment of hairy ethical quandaries. Who should have access to these new missiles? What if a technical error were to result in friendly fire? Can he make peace with his technology being used to end somebody’s life?

Dandzbergs claims that he has come to terms with these issues. He says he takes solace in the belief that his motivations are pure: to protect the US and its European allies.

By his logic, autonomous weapons are an inevitable part of the future, so he might as well be the one who builds them. “I choose not to shy away from the emergence of modern conflict as a global issue. I simply don’t have a lot of respect for people that do,” he says. “I want to be part of it. I want to move with the future of warfare.”

But others who have found themselves in a similar position say it’s difficult to know in advance how somebody will deal with the knowledge that their invention has killed people.

McKenzie-Wilson, the Unicorn Mafia director, launched a drone startup in his early twenties. Initially, he had meant for the drones to evacuate wounded soldiers, but after a missile company invested, his startup retooled its technology to drop payloads too. He left around the same time.

“I don’t think I was emotionally mature enough,” he says. “[Dandzbergs] will realize at some point. He’ll either have an issue with it or he won’t.”

Polemos Industries announced itself publicly on November 5 with a glossy social media video. Set to the ticking of a clock, it featured a set of four missiles fired into the sky from ground launchers, then blowing a convoy of four military vehicles to smithereens.

The video climbed to the top of the trending page on X. In the responses, a couple of people tagged Palmer Luckey, cofounder of Anduril, who was 19 when he started his first company. “Coming for him,” Dandzbergs responded.

Dandzbergs recognizes that people are likely to dismiss him for his age. But he considers youth one of his biggest advantages. “Please don’t say I’m sacrificing my childhood,” he says. “This is the childhood I dreamed of.”

Mark Noble Reveals Important Change Behind West Ham’s Transfer Plans

📅 Nov 20, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Mark Noble has provided an interesting insight into how West Ham United’s recruitment operation is working under Scott Parker, revealing that Nils Koppen is in regular contact with the coaching staff about the players the club needs.

Speaking in an interview with West Ham’s official website, Noble explained that the Director of Player Recruitment is arriving at Rush Green as early as 6.30am, watching training and discussing the squad with Parker and his coaches.

With the January transfer window approaching and West Ham desperate to secure promotion, it’s encouraging to hear that recruitment discussions are taking place. But for me, the most important part of Noble’s revelation has very little to do with what time Koppen arrives for work.

Quite frankly, whether Koppen arrives at half six in the morning is neither here nor there.

Much of the analytical and recruitment work can be carried out perfectly well from wherever somebody can plug into the internet.

What I find far more important is the open dialogue Noble has described.

He explained that Koppen watches training and that the coaching staff speak openly with him about the players they believe West Ham need.

Koppen then works with the club’s scouts and analysts in the background, allowing Parker and Noble to concentrate on coaching.

That strikes me as a huge plus and potentially a massive step in the right direction.

It also appears to represent a departure from the way things have operated previously, when questions were regularly raised about whether recruitment decisions matched the manager’s requirements.

As we discussed following Parker’s talkSPORT interview, the manager is absolutely right to concentrate on improving the players already at his disposal rather than becoming consumed by January transfers.

But that only works if the recruitment department understands precisely what he needs.

We know from our recent dialogue with a club insider that Koppen has begun looking for another striker, and Noble’s comments suggest those discussions are taking place alongside the coaching staff’s assessment of the squad.

Ultimately, I’m far more interested in whether Koppen signs the right players than whether he’s the first person through the door at Rush Green.

But if the people coaching the team and those recruiting for it are finally speaking openly about what West Ham need, that has to be encouraging.
