Commerce approved sales of more than 879,000 AI chips to Chinese companies, Rep. Mast says

📅 2026-11-19  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

The subagency of the Commerce Department that regulates and enforces exports approved the sale of more than 879,000 AI chips to Chinese companies including Alibaba and Tencent as of May 2026, according to documents released Thursday by the House Foreign Affairs Committee.

Some of those approvals, made by Commerce's Bureau of Industry and Security, included sales to companies designated by the Department of Defense as "Chinese Military Companies," according to the documents. From December through May, there had been 3.4 million requested sales of U.S. AI chips to China, the committee reported, citing those made by Nvidia and Advanced Micro Devices.

"Over the past month, the administration's handling of chip export controls has put our national security and technology edge at risk. The Bureau of Industry and Security has failed to enforce the rules, and Congress must now conduct rigorous oversight of the administration's policies," Rep. Gregory Meeks, D-N.Y., the top Democrat on the House Foreign Affairs Committee, said at a meeting of the panel Thursday at which members voted to release the documents.

Spokespersons for Nvidia and AMD said their companies comply with U.S. export control laws.

Nvidia argued it was following the Commerce Department regulations that were in effect at the time.

"The review and licensing process spanned more than half a year, with approvals subject to strict conditions and limited volumes. Despite receiving some licenses, we have since stopped shipping H200s to China," Nvidia spokesperson John Rizzo told CNBC.

On Tuesday, Nvidia reported $2.4 billion in quarterly revenue from H200 and related chip sales in China. In total, customers based in China and Hong Kong made up about 9.5% of all revenue.

"AMD complies with applicable U.S. export control laws and regulations and maintains rigorous processes to ensure compliance across our global operations," AMD said through a spokesperson.

The approval of 879,000 AI chip is "not that many," Bernstein Senior Analyst Stacy Rasgon told CNBC, adding that this news is not surprising.

"It doesn't sound to me like they're approving everything they got willy nilly," Rasgon said of the Commerce Department. With 879,000 approvals out of 3.4 million requested AI chip sales, he added, "There was a process in place, and it sounds like they approved about one out of every four."

Despite a rapid succession of China export controls on AI chips that started under the Biden administration in 2022, Nvidia and AMD both re-engineered some chips to comply and be sold legally in China. And in December, Trump said Nvidia could ship H200s, its older generation Hopper chips, to "approved customers in China" if the U.S. got a 25% cut of sales.

The U.S. then licensed Nvidia H200 sales to Alibaba, Tencent, ByteDance and other Chinese companies, although sales were limited because Beijing didn't approve large-scale imports, chip analyst Patrick Moorhead of Moor Insights & Strategy told CNBC last week.

H200 sales numbers remained low due to those restrictions on China's end. Nvidia said H200 shipments to China made up less than 1% of data center revenue for the quarter that ended in July.

"In the grand context of the size of this market and how much they're shipping, it's a rounding error. It's tiny," Rasgon told CNBC.

The committee cited the Export Control Reform Act as justification for releasing the data. The panel said the law allows it to declassify and release export data if it "determines that the withholding of the following information is contrary to the national interest."

The Commerce Department did not respond to a request for comment.The license requests released by the Foreign Affairs Committee include requests to sell to major firms including Bytedance, Tesla (Shanghai) and Baidu.

The release of documents comes in the wake of Jensen Huang's resignation from the chip supplier Nvidia after news surfaced that he had several extramarital affairs, including one with a suspected Chinese spy that led to concerns about Nvidia chips being diverted to China.

In the weeks since, Republicans and Democrats in Congress have called for legislation to be passed that would impose stricter export controls on the sale of advanced AI chips to China and other adversaries.

"The license information we are disclosing today will help inform the American public about how BIS has handled licenses for Nvidia H200 and equivalent AMD chips to China. The public has the right to know how these decisions were made," Meeks said.

Mast said two of the firms listed in Nvidia license applications to BIS — Maginfra and New Matrix — are suspected "smuggling fronts." The New York Times reported in November that both companies had empty offices and had ties to companies under national security sanctions.

"Both are fake companies. If BIS approved the sale of these chips to these firms, obviously that is of tremendous concern to us — especially given that President Trump made clear last year that the sale of any AI chips to China will have very real national security conditions," Mast said at the Thursday meeting.

Maginfra and New Matrix did not reply to requests for comment.

Shares of AMD fell Thursday after the investment firm Hunterbrook Media issued a report alleging the company "has its own China problem." The report claims that "after the U.S. blacklisted Chinese server maker IEIT Systems, the company kept getting newer AMD firmware, which enables AMD chips to run in servers."

CNBC did not independently verify this information.

Fallout from the Huang scandal on Capitol Hill has intensified in the last week, as lawmakers question the degree to which the former head of Nvidia's relationship with the suspected Chinese spy could have jeopardized national security and American AI dominance.

Huang is due for a closed-door transcribed interview with the House Foreign Affairs panel and two other committees on Dec. 1. Nvidia general counsel Tim Teter is scheduled to appear before the panels Dec. 2.

Senate banking committee Chair Tim Scott, R-S.C., announced on CNBC's "Squawk Box" on Wednesday that Huang and Commerce Secretary Howard Lutnick had also agreed to testify.

Lutnick has come under fire over his relationship to Huang and for his failure to take action on Megaspeed, the Singaporean company where Alice Huang, with whom the former Nvidia CEO had an affair, was an executive until 2025. U.S. investigators suspect Megaspeed diverted Nvidia chips to China.

Amid that criticism, the Commerce Department on Monday issued an interim final rule to close a loophole that has allowed Chinese companies lease Nvidia's advancing computing power from data centers in third controls.

The rule was "written by and at the direction of" the Treasury Department and the White House National Security Council, BIS said in a press release.

— Megan Cassella, Daren Fonda and Kif Leswing contributed to this report.

Lumivero Opens SharpCloud POLI Beta for Project Portfolio Decisions

📅 Nov 19, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

SharpCloud’s POLI beta uses portfolio data to answer questions and model changes, but keeps approval with the people running the projects.

Lumivero has opened a beta of POLI, a set of AI agents built into its SharpCloud portfolio management software. The company introduced POLI on November 16 and says beta access began November 17 for customers and prospects. The agents can answer questions about projects and risks or explore the consequences of a proposed change. A person has to confirm a change before it is applied to the portfolio.

The design targets project portfolios, not investment trading. A company running several factory upgrades, for example, might use it to ask which work depends on a delayed delivery. The answer depends on connections the team has already recorded in SharpCloud. If the shared supplier or affected milestone is missing, the model cannot be relied on to know about it.

Teams typically keep schedules, risks and dependencies in separate systems. A project may appear on track in its own schedule even though a prerequisite is slipping elsewhere. SharpCloud gives teams a way to represent those relationships explicitly. In January, Lumivero announced its acquisition of SharpCloud, identifying portfolio visualization and the ability to model how one change might affect multiple projects as reasons for the deal.

POLI works on that connected portfolio model. Its five default agents are Getting Started, Comparison, What-If Impact, Story Summary and Product Knowledge. They cover different jobs, from helping a new user get oriented to comparing items or summarizing a portfolio view. Comparison can make the differences between projects easier to see. What-If Impact asks a different question: if a project assumption changes, what other parts of the modeled portfolio could be affected?

The distinction matters because dependencies are often more useful than a ranking. If a delayed equipment installation pushes back the training associated with it, the important result is not just that a milestone moved. It is which people need to adjust their plans and which assumptions they need to verify. Scenario analysis helps identify those questions without asking the system to decide what the business should do.

Lumivero says What-If Impact is read-only and does not change the data it evaluates. The company also says POLI respects existing user permissions, shows the basis for its answers and leaves proposed changes for people to approve. AI is opt-in per SharpCloud instance. These are vendor-described controls, not independent findings about how accurately the agents work on messy or incomplete data.

Human approval is most useful when an explanation can be checked against the original records. A plausible answer may use a valid but outdated estimate, or a dependency that no longer applies. A reviewer needs to see which records the assistant used and know who maintains them. An approval step provides a point of control, but it does not fix stale inputs or make every recommendation correct.

Lumivero’s own capital project decision-making report says few surveyed respondents would act on an AI recommendation without significant review. The survey comes from the vendor and does not verify POLI’s performance. It does, however, illustrate the adoption problem the product is meant to address: teams want help pulling together scattered information while retaining responsibility for any decision based on it.

Early users can test the agents on familiar examples where the project team already knows the outcome, then compare the answers with the underlying records and repeat the test with a different access level. That would show where POLI helps reduce the time spent cross-checking schedules, and where missing records still require manual investigation. The initial beta is best understood as a way to query and test a portfolio model, not a replacement for the people accountable for it. Lumivero lists separate analysis and controls modules on its roadmap for the first half of 2027; those modules are not part of the current beta.

Anthropic IPO Draws Protests Over AI Safety and Healthcare

📅 Nov 19, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

ACT UP NY and Ban RSI organized separate demonstrations in New York and San Francisco on November 19, 2026, as Anthropic entered public markets. The actions challenged AI in healthcare and recursive self-improvement, while a separate campaign pressed financial institutions over AI investment and the use of retirement savings.

ACT UP NY called supporters to 4 Times Square at 8:30 a.m. ET, where Anthropic was holding its Nasdaq IPO event. Its action notice asked members to hold an AI Out Of Our Healthcare banner, distribute flyers and potentially speak at the rally and to reporters.

ACT UP’s notice said some of the practices it opposes have already begun in hospitals and doctors’ offices. Its concerns include AI replacing live medical screening or hospital staff, influencing treatment and outcomes, training on health data, and racial, gender and anti-LGBTQ bias.

Ban RSI’s rally page scheduled a separate gathering outside Anthropic’s office at 500 Howard Street in San Francisco. The event listing set it for 11 a.m. to noon Pacific.

The listing subsequently showed 164 accounts in its Went category, a host-platform RSVP status rather than a verified crowd count. Ban RSI listed former AI researchers Jacob Coxon and Daniel Kokotajlo and AI safety educator Rob Miles as scheduled speakers.

Ban RSI defines recursive self-improvement as AI doing the work of building more powerful AI. It wants large AI companies prohibited from using systems more powerful than those available at the rally for AI research, ideally through an international treaty. Its proposed enforcement mechanism would require more powerful systems to have safeguards blocking such research even internally, red-team testing of those safeguards, and exceptions for monitoring and safety research that clearly do not increase AI capability.

The group is not demanding a unilateral pause. It wants Anthropic to announce concrete, verifiable steps toward avoiding recursive self-improvement, potentially including urging other AI companies to follow suit or lobbying for legislation.

Anthropic’s final prospectus describes closing the loop as a structural advantage: successive Claude versions assist in building the next through experiments, training-data generation and processing, evaluations, safety audits, and code writing and review.

An Anthropic Institute article says full recursive self-improvement, meaning an AI system autonomously designing and developing its own successor, has not arrived and is not inevitable. It also says such a capability could increase the risk of humans losing control. The article identifies a remaining gap in Claude’s judgment about which goals and experiments to pursue, even as the system takes on more engineering and research work.

The company says it would expect to slow down or temporarily pause if other developers at or near the frontier did so verifiably. Its proposed conditions include multiple well-resourced labs in multiple countries and systems that can confirm compliance.

Anthropic argues that a unilateral pause would accomplish less and could change the industry front-runner. That creates a narrow point of agreement with Ban RSI, which also rejects a unilateral-pause demand, but a difference over the immediate obligation: the protesters want Anthropic to announce verifiable steps itself, while the company’s stated expectation to pause depends on reciprocal action.

A separate joint petition sponsored by Action Center on Race and the Economy, Stop Funding Billionaires, Climate Defenders and other organizations calls for a boycott of Anthropic’s IPO, a pause on all AI and data-center investments, and a pledge not to use taxpayer or retirement money for an AI bailout. It is addressed to bank executives underwriting or facilitating the offering, state and local financial officers, asset managers and institutional investors, including university endowments and foundations.

The financial objections also turn attention to who can control Anthropic following its initial public offering. The final prospectus priced 980 million Class A shares at $83 each, an $81.34 billion offering. It provides for a single Class F share held by the founders’ Founder LLC to carry votes equal to 50.1% of general voting power, while the Long-Term Benefit Trust generally can elect and remove the Class T directors who constitute a majority of the board. Class A shares carry one vote each.

Those arrangements concentrate general voting power with the founders’ vehicle and board-selection power with the trust. The IPO therefore leaves a paired accountability question: whether Anthropic’s development practices can be verifiably constrained, and whether that governance structure will uphold the company’s public-benefit purpose of responsible AI development for humanity’s long-term benefit.

Form & Function Launches Lever to Give AI Assistants Shared Business Context

📅 Nov 19, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Form & Function Consulting has launched Lever, an AI platform designed to give professional services firms a consistent view of their operations across disconnected systems. Announced on November 12, the product combines operational data with company-specific business definitions so leaders can ask questions about staffing, revenue forecasts and project profitability through conversational interfaces.

The interesting part is not another chatbot answering financial questions. It is the work required before those answers become useful. A sales system can record a promising deal while a delivery team sees insufficient capacity to fulfill it. An AI assistant needs more than access to both databases to explain what that combination means for the business.

According to the launch announcement, Form & Function starts with information from customer relationship management, professional services automation and finance systems. It also gathers documents and the knowledge employees use to interpret those records. Its Lev chatbot supports stakeholder interviews that capture how a firm actually operates.

That onboarding feeds a context layer describing how work is sold, staffed, delivered and measured. The goal is to connect questions that otherwise fall between departments: whether the sales pipeline supports a hiring decision, which engagements offer the best margins, or how a delayed investment would affect projected cash flow.

This distinction matters in services businesses because similarly named metrics can conceal different assumptions. Available capacity might exclude time spent selling, while a revenue forecast might depend on both a deal’s likelihood of closing and when the resulting work can be delivered. A plausible answer based on the wrong definition can be more misleading than an obvious error.

The company’s Lever product page demonstrates Lev answering questions, generating visual output and working inside Slack. It also shows access through other AI clients, including Claude and ChatGPT, using Model Context Protocol, or MCP. Rather than requiring every interface to reconstruct the firm’s business logic, the approach supplies a shared foundation for those interactions.

The launch positioned Lever within operating-partner advisory engagements. The current product page also describes standalone availability. Pricing and detailed deployment requirements are not disclosed there, so the announcement should not be read as a self-service rollout with a published subscription price.

The broader move toward shared business meaning is also visible in Collate’s Ontology Studio announcement. Lever takes a narrower approach centered on professional services operations and consultant-assisted onboarding, rather than treating business context as a generic layer that every organization can adopt unchanged.

Form & Function reports that one client identified additional delivery capacity sufficient to support approximately $1.8 million in incremental annual revenue without adding employees. The release also describes an improvement in projected gross margins. Those are potential operating gains identified through analysis, not evidence that the client has already collected that additional revenue.

The firm’s technical discussion of semantic layers provides more context. In a company-run comparison, it tested five business questions five times each, contrasting access to raw BigQuery data with a semantic model built using Malloy. The structured approach performed better on questions requiring business definitions and relationships between tables.

That is a useful illustration of the mechanism, but it is a small vendor-run test. It does not establish perfect accuracy across arbitrary questions, changing datasets or every customer’s deployment. Defining metrics in advance reduces ambiguity; it does not eliminate the need to maintain those definitions and review consequential decisions.

Lever’s launch highlights a practical shift in enterprise AI: connecting a model to data is only the beginning. Someone must decide what the data means, resolve conflicting definitions and keep that interpretation current as contracts, staffing policies and business processes change.

For services firms, the potential benefit is a shorter path from operational records to a decision about capacity or profitability. The lasting test will be whether that shared context stays reliable as the business evolves, and whether identified opportunities turn into measurable results rather than simply more persuasive answers.

Tanium AgentSight Brings Shadow AI Into the Endpoint Inventory

📅 Nov 19, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Tanium’s AgentSight launch gives security teams a view of AI tools installed on employee devices, while agent activity tracking and broader enforcement remain on the roadmap.

Tanium introduced AgentSight at its Converge conference in Las Vegas this week, giving security teams a way to inventory AI software running on employee devices. The company says the first capabilities are available now in the Tanium console. They identify local models, coding agents, AI extensions and Model Context Protocol (MCP) servers across Windows, macOS and Linux, then associate each item with a device and the person who installed it.

That launch is narrower than the long-term product Tanium described. The first release answers what is installed and where. The company is still developing parts of the system that would explain what an agent did, flag unusual behavior or apply a policy across many devices. For organizations choosing how to govern AI, the distinction matters: a complete inventory is valuable, but it does not establish that an agent’s actions are authorized.

An enterprise can govern a company-wide AI subscription and still have limited visibility into the assistants staff install on their own workstations. An approved coding tool might sit alongside several extensions, a local model and a connector to another service. A software inventory lets IT see which devices are running those components and gives security staff a starting point for tracking down who installed them. It also helps explain why approving one service does not automatically account for every tool that ends up in a workflow.

Tanium says its initial visibility is available in on-premises and air-gapped environments as well. That matters for teams whose devices cannot send telemetry to a cloud service. The company also says cost tracking in Tanium Asset shows where AI software is deployed and what it costs. An endpoint view is useful for these questions, but it is not a substitute for records from browser-based services, cloud systems or the applications an agent uses remotely.

In the launch announcement, Tanium described a Converge demonstration of a Shadow AI page in Atlas where security teams can see changes in the inventory and ask questions in plain language. The company described additional plans to rank findings, provide explanations, and tie agent activity to the originating session and prompt. The demonstration and roadmap should not be mistaken for proof that those features are generally available or that they cover every agent or service.

Tanium also plans to let security teams define policies centrally and detect violations before they turn on enforcement. That sequence matters operationally. A local tool that looks unusual in the inventory might be part of a legitimate engineering or accessibility workflow. Reviewing those cases with the people who own the affected systems can help organizations distinguish authorized exceptions from unmanaged software without disrupting routine work.

The voluntary NIST AI Risk Management Framework puts AI risk in the context of how systems are used, not simply what is installed. For an enterprise, that means linking a device inventory to the people, permissions and business processes behind the entries. It also means deciding what information should be collected and who can review it, especially if future activity records include prompts that may contain confidential work.

The near-term test for AgentSight is whether security teams can keep an accurate inventory as employees adopt and change tools. The more demanding test is whether businesses can connect that visibility to useful decisions: which tools are approved, where an exception is justified, and who should review behavior that falls outside policy. Tanium has shipped the first part of that work. The rest will depend on both the product roadmap and how companies put it to use.

"Nico Paz, ma è più forte di me… non scherziamo. Un fenomeno".

AWS Lets AgentCore Agents Buy Marketplace Products Through AWS Billing

📅 Nov 19, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Amazon Web Services announced on November 19, 2026 that Amazon Bedrock AgentCore payments can now settle purchases from x402-enabled AWS Marketplace sellers through AWS Billing. AI agents can buy software, APIs, data and content per request in US dollars, with charges added to the agent developer’s AWS bill.

For those purchases, the integration removes the need for stablecoin wallets or API keys. An optional AWS Marketplace connector for AgentCore Gateway also lets agents discover paid products and endpoints, while developers keep spending limits, seller permissions and audit records within their AWS accounts.

A purchase starts when an agent calls a seller endpoint without payment. The seller responds with HTTP 402 Payment Required and places a signed quote in the PAYMENT-REQUIRED header. The agent forwards the quote to AgentCore payments through the ProcessPayment API using the AWS_BILLING_X402 payment type. If the session has enough remaining budget, the service returns a payment token; the agent retries the request with that token in the PAYMENT-SIGNATURE header and receives the content.

At launch, AWS Billing supports the x402 exact scheme, under which the seller quotes a fixed price for each request. AWS Marketplace verifies the quote’s signature against the seller’s registered key, rejecting a quote whose price has been altered. The post says payment tokens remain valid for about five minutes, so the quote, payment and redemption steps occur in a single pass.

The first purchase from a seller creates an AWS Marketplace agreement for the developer’s AWS account, and later purchases are tracked against it. Products appear as Marketplace line items, with settlement running asynchronously before charges appear on the monthly AWS bill. Purchases are also recorded in AWS CloudTrail and AWS Cost Explorer.

Setup requires a Payment Manager with an AWS Billing connector and an AWS Marketplace service-linked role. For each task, developers can create a payment session with a spending limit and give the agent its session ID. If the remaining budget is insufficient, the payment call fails and the developer is not charged.

AgentCore policy supports additional spending limits and approved-seller lists. Organizations using Private Marketplace restrict agents to the products that private catalog allows. The open-source sample also separates permissions: its session role can set budgets but cannot pay, while its agent role can pay but cannot create sessions, preventing the agent from increasing its own budget.

The optional gateway connector exposes three read-only tools: search listings, get a listing and list fulfillment options. Listing search returns only endpoints that accept x402, while the fulfillment call supplies the seller’s endpoint URL and accepted payment protocols. Developers can bypass discovery by setting a seller URL directly; the agent then pays only that endpoint.

AWS’s walkthrough uses a Strands research agent answering a financial analyst’s question about agentic-payment announcements outside the model’s training data. The agent finds Tavily’s live-search listing, requests its x402 endpoints, obtains a quote and pays for one request. Its answer includes sources and identifies what it purchased and how much it cost.

The sample defaults to a $0.05 session budget and a $0.02 cap per request. In AWS’s documented test, the agent compared two listings before completing one $0.01 payment.

For AWS customers, the launch connects product discovery and per-request payment to established cloud billing and governance controls. An agent’s purchasing autonomy is therefore bounded by the specific session budget, approved sellers, role permissions and Marketplace catalog access its developers configure.

Multi-Turn Agentic Reinforcement Learning with Tunix and Gemma 4 on Google TPUs

📅 Nov 19, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

In our previous posts - Easy FunctionGemma finetuning with Tunix on Google TPUs and Autonomous LLM post-training with Tunix on TPUs - we showed how Google Tunix streamlines Supervised Fine-Tuning (SFT) and single-turn Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO) on Cloud TPUs. Real-world AI agents, however, rarely finish their job in a single turn. They have to reason over partial observations, invoke tools, observe environment feedback, and adapt their strategy across multiple steps. So in this post, we will walk through how to train a multi-turn, tool-using LLM agent using Tunix's agentic reinforcement learning pipeline on Cloud TPU v6e-8. You can find the complete code in this GitHub repository.

We will revisit the Plane Strike board game from an earlier TensorFlow/JAX reinforcement learning blog, replacing the custom MLP policy with Gemma 4 E2B in thinking mode on a single Cloud TPU v6e-8 host. Plane Strike is played on a 6x6 grid (A1 to F6) where one 8-cell plane is hidden in one of 4 orientations. Each turn, the agent sees the result of its previous strike and the raw visible board (. untried, X hit, O miss), thinks through candidate placements, and calls the strike(cell="...") tool to strike one cell. The game ends when all 8 plane cells are hit, or after 23 strikes.

Sorry, your browser doesn't support playback for this video

Although google/gemma-4-E2B-it has decent spatial reasoning and tool calling capabilities, out of the box, it wins only 9.4% of games on held-out boards (averaging 22.44 strikes). After 100 steps of agentic GRPO with Tunix, its win rate reaches 95% while cutting the average game length to ~16 strikes.

We set up the multi-turn RL pipeline in four modular components:

Let's look at the key steps for each piece.

In env.py, we subclass Tunix's BaseTaskEnv to implement PlaneStrikeEnv. Each environment instance receives a task dictionary with a seed that fixes the hidden plane position so all 8 rollouts in a GRPO group play the exact same board:

On each turn, _step_impl evaluates the struck cell index against the hidden board, updates the visible grid, computes the step reward (summed into the trajectory-level reward over the episode), and returns EnvStepResult(observation, reward, done, info).

For standard multi-tool agents, tunix.rl.agentic also provides ready-to-use ToolEnvironment, ToolAgent and ToolManager abstractions out of the box so we don't have to write custom message/observation plumbing unless we need custom turn control.

In Gemma 4, tool schemas are declared in the system prompt inside <|tool>declaration:..., and a model turn that calls a tool ends with:

<|tool_response> is one of Gemma 4's native stop tokens: the model pauses there and waits for response:strike{value:<|"|>...<|"|>}, then continues generating its next thought and tool call within the same assistant turn.

In strike_tool.py, we define StrikeTool(BaseTool) and subclass Tunix's Gemma4ChatTemplateParser to append the tool declaration to the system prompt and render "tool" messages as in-turn responses:

In agent.py, PlaneStrikeAgent(ConversationAgentBase) translates between environment observations and model messages.

When the model finishes a turn, tool_strike ignores everything inside <|channel>thought... and parses the final answer after ; only a final answer with a single valid strike call counts as a valid board coordinate (otherwise INVALID_ACTION is returned). If the model stopped at <|tool_response>, the next board observation is appended with role="tool" so the model continues its turn:

In train_planestrike.py, we configure Tunix's asynchronous vllm rollout engine, RLEngine, and GRPOLearner:

Multi-turn agentic RL is often bottlenecked by variable-length rollout generation, so we set rollout_vllm_server_mode to true to enable continuous asynchronous multi-turn rollouts via vllm-tpu and enable_prefix_caching to true to true to cache the growing multi-turn conversation history. Since we are using a v6e-8 single host, the actor model, the reference model, and the rollout workers share the 8 chips.

We then pass our actor, tokenizer, ClusterConfig, GRPOConfig (loss_algo="gspo-token", advantage_estimator="rloo", exact_token_continuity=True to preserve exact token IDs across multi-turn rollouts without retokenization drift), agent, and environment into RLEngine and PlaneStrikeGRPOLearner:

All of these enable us to launch the 100-step training job with:

Over 100 steps (8 boards x 8 games per step), the agent learns both reliable tool calling and spatial deduction:

With Tunix's tunix.rl.agentic abstractions, vllm-tpu, and GRPOLearner, multi-turn, tool-using agentic RL on Cloud TPUs is easy and efficient. Check out the planestrike-gemma4-rl GitHub repository and read the Tunix documentation to train your own agentic model with Tunix and Google TPUs.

Python Day 4: Bitwise Operators and Ternary Operator

📅 Nov 19, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

The result is 1 when both bits are different. If both bits are the same, the result is 0.

Binary 001 means: (0 * 4) + (0 * 2) + (1 * 1) = 1

Because both numbers are the same, the XOR result is 0.

The Bitwise NOT operator reverses the bits of a number.

In Python, the result of Bitwise NOT can be calculated using this formula:

Shift operators are used to move the bits of a number to the left or right.

The left shift operator moves the bits towards the left. For positive integers, each shift by one position multiplies the number by 2.

The right shift operator moves the bits towards the right. For positive integers, each shift by one position divides the number by 2 and takes the floor value.

The ternary operator is also called the conditional expression in Python.

It allows us to write a simple if-else condition in a single line.

value_if_true if condition else value_if_false

GPS, Timezone and IP Are Three Different Things: Testing Geo Behaviour on Real Phones

📅 Nov 19, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Posted on Nov 19 • Originally published at robotactions.com

Short answer: you set your test phone's GPS to Paris, and your checkout still shows prices in Canadian dollars. That is because GPS is only one of the signals that say where a phone is. The server it talks to reads its IP address. The app reads its clock. The app store reads the account. If you want to see what a user in Paris sees, those signals have to agree on one city, and each one is set in a different place. This post goes through each signal, what it controls, how to set it on a real Android phone or iPhone, and how to check that nothing leaked.

Two more signals are worth knowing because they cannot be changed by any of the above:

Most "it works in testing but not for users in Brazil" bugs come from testing one signal and assuming the others followed.

GPS and API responses can be faked from inside your own test setup: you mock the location, or you intercept the response from your geo-lookup service and return a different country. That covers anything your app decides.

It does not cover decisions made before your code runs:

To test those, the traffic has to actually leave from that country. That means a proxy.

Use this whether you test on your own phones or in a device cloud.

In both cases the proxy applies to that Wi-Fi network only. Mobile data still goes out directly.

If your provider gives you a SOCKS5 proxy, prefer the socks5h:// form where your tool accepts it. With socks5h, DNS lookups are done by the proxy, in the target country. With plain socks5, the phone or your tool resolves hostnames locally first, which can send it to a CDN node near you, not near the exit IP, and quietly undo the test.

A phone's proxy setting is a request, not a rule. Apps and system services that open their own connections can ignore it:

So "the browser shows a New York IP" is not proof that your app's traffic left from New York. If the behaviour you are testing comes from one of these paths, check that path specifically: watch the connections the phone opens while you use the app, and look for any that went straight to the internet.

RobotActions runs real Android and iOS devices in our cloud, and we added proxy routing for this kind of test. You bring your own HTTP(S) or SOCKS5 proxy from any provider and pay them directly. We do not sell proxies. Paste it once in Settings, Proxies (in any of the usual formats, including a provider's host:port:user:pass line or a whole curl -x command), click Test to see the exit IP and city, and save it. The password is stored encrypted and never shown again. On a device, Location & Network applies it, checks it before the phone changes, and can move the GPS and timezone to the proxy's exit city in one click. On Android the exit IP is also read back from inside the phone. Releasing the device removes the proxy and puts GPS and timezone back. Proxy routing is on the paid plans.

AI agents get the same thing as MCP tools:

On iPhone, fullDevice: true is opt-in and sends every app and system service through your proxy, not just the ones that honour the proxy setting. While it is on, UDP traffic such as calls and some games stops. ios_proxy_leak_check watches for 5 to 60 seconds while you use the app and lists the connections that went around the proxy. Both proxy and timezone are cleared automatically when the device is released.

Your Card Game's Turns Need a State Machine, Not Callbacks

📅 Nov 19, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

I built a small deckbuilder for a devchallenge jam, and like every first-time card game dev, I started with the thing that feels natural in Godot: signals and callbacks. card_played fires, which triggers on_card_played() on three other systems, one of which emits mana_spent, which triggers a UI update and also checks win conditions. It worked great for about two weeks. Then I added triggered abilities ("whenever a creature dies, draw a card") and the whole thing turned into a debugging nightmare where I genuinely could not tell you what order things happened in.

This post is about the rewrite: modeling turn structure, phases, and triggered abilities as an explicit finite-state machine (FSM), and why that unlocked undo/replay almost for free.

The core issue with signal-driven turn logic isn't that signals are bad — it's that turn order becomes implicit and distributed. Nobody owns "what happens next." Every system that connects to card_played is independently deciding when it's allowed to react, and Godot's signal dispatch order depends on connection order, which depends on scene tree instantiation order, which depends on... nothing you want to be debugging at 2am.

Concretely, this breaks down in three ways once your game grows past trivial complexity:

The fix is to stop treating turn progression as "things that happen when events fire" and start treating it as a graph of named states with explicit, enumerable transitions.

I modeled the turn as a strict phase sequence, with each phase being a distinct state object rather than a flag:

gdscript enum Phase { UNTAP, UPKEEP, DRAW, MAIN, COMBAT, MAIN_2, END, CLEANUP, }

class_name TurnStateMachine extends RefCounted

var current_phase: Phase = Phase.UNTAP var transition_table := { Phase.UNTAP: Phase.UPKEEP, Phase.UPKEEP: Phase.DRAW, Phase.DRAW: Phase.MAIN, Phase.MAIN: Phase.COMBAT, Phase.COMBAT: Phase.MAIN_2, Phase.MAIN_2: Phase.END, Phase.END: Phase.CLEANUP, Phase.CLEANUP: Phase.UNTAP, }

signal phase_entered(phase: Phase) signal phase_exited(phase: Phase)

func advance() -> void: phase_exited.emit(current_phase) current_phase = transition_table[current_phase] phase_entered.emit(current_phase)

This alone doesn't look that different from what people already do. The real difference is the rule I enforced everywhere else in the codebase: nothing is allowed to mutate game state outside of a phase transition or an explicit action resolution. No system is allowed to "just react" whenever it feels like it. If something wants to happen, it has to go through the machine.

The thing that actually broke my original signal-based design was triggered abilities. "Whenever a creature dies, draw a card" sounds simple until three creatures die simultaneously from one board wipe, and two of those deaths also trigger abilities that kill other creatures.

The fix: triggers don't execute immediately. They get pushed onto a resolution queue, and the state machine has a dedicated RESOLVING_TRIGGERS state that drains the queue one at a time, re-checking for new triggers after each resolution (this mirrors how Magic: The Gathering's actual rules engine handles the stack, and for good reason — it's a solved problem).

gdscript var trigger_queue: Array[TriggeredAbility] = []

func queue_trigger(ability: TriggeredAbility) -> void: trigger_queue.append(ability) if current_phase != Phase.RESOLVING_TRIGGERS: _enter_resolution_state()

func _enter_resolution_state() -> void: var previous_phase := current_phase current_phase = Phase.RESOLVING_TRIGGERS while not trigger_queue.is_empty(): var ability := trigger_queue.pop_front() ability.resolve() # resolving may queue MORE triggers — loop keeps draining current_phase = previous_phase

This single change killed an entire category of bugs. Because resolution is a loop inside one state rather than a chain of emitted signals, there's no ambiguity about ordering, and I can log the queue at any point to see exactly what's pending.

Once every state mutation happens through a small set of commands rather than ad-hoc function calls, undo and replay stop being special features and become a natural consequence of the architecture. I used a lightweight command pattern where every action produces a reversible record:

gdscript class_name GameCommand extends RefCounted

func apply(state: GameState) -> void: push_error("override me")

func revert(state: GameState) -> void: push_error("override me")

class DrawCardCommand extends GameCommand: var player_id: int var drawn_card_id: String

The turn engine keeps a flat history list. "Undo" is just revert() on the last command. Replay for the jam's leaderboard verification is just replaying the command list from an empty GameState and checking the hash at the end matches. I didn't design the command log for replay specifically — it fell out of needing undo during the "take back a misclick" phase of playtesting, and the replay feature was maybe 40 lines of glue on top.

To be fair about the cost: this architecture is more boilerplate upfront. Every single action — drawing a card, dealing damage, tapping a permanent — needs its own command class instead of a quick one-liner in a signal handler. For a jam with a 72-hour timer, that felt expensive on day one.

It also makes animation sequencing trickier, because visual feedback wants to happen during state changes, not strictly before or after. I ended up having commands emit a visual_hint payload that the presentation layer consumes asynchronously, decoupled from the actual state mutation timing — the state machine doesn't wait on animations, ever.

And if your game genuinely has no triggered abilities, no undo requirement, and a fixed linear turn order, you may not need any of this. Signals are fine for a tic-tac-toe clone. The complexity is earned, not default.

If you've shipped a card or board game with any kind of "stack" of triggered effects — how did you handle simultaneous triggers and ordering? Did you go full FSM, or find a lighter-weight trick that worked for your scope? I'd genuinely like to know what breaks first in other people's designs, because mine was ordering, and I suspect that's not universal.

How to Add Web Search to OpenClaw (Free, No API Key)

📅 Nov 19, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Posted on Nov 19 • Originally published at agentsearchhq.com

OpenClaw is an open-source AI assistant that runs on your own computer and answers you in the chat apps you already use. Its web_search tool needs a search provider, and most of the built-in choices want an API key before the first query.

This guide adds web search to OpenClaw with the AgentSearch provider plugin, @agentsearchhq/openclaw-web-search. It works right away with no API key, no signup and no wallet: up to 25 searches a day per IP. If you need more, give it a small wallet and each search costs $0.003 in USDC on Base over x402, with no daily cap.

Every command below was run against OpenClaw 2026.11.5 and plugin 0.1.0 in a clean, isolated home directory: install, provider selection, plugin config and the provider check. We didn't run a live free search for this post; the free path is covered by the plugin's test suite.

The plugin registers one provider, id agentsearch, for OpenClaw's web_search tool. Each search returns up to 5 ranked results with a title, URL, snippet, publish date and site name.

There's no account at any tier. Paid search uses x402: the API answers with a 402 price, the plugin signs a small USDC payment on Base, and the payment settles only when a usable answer comes back. Errors and empty result sets aren't charged. You don't need ETH for gas.

The plugin isn't on ClawHub yet, so install it from npm with the npm: prefix:

OpenClaw warns that npm packages are outside ClawHub review and asks you to confirm, then asks you to accept the plugin's capabilities (it declares one: web-search: agentsearch). In a script or any shell without a prompt, pass both answers as flags:

You should see Installed plugin: agentsearch and Saved for the next Gateway start. OpenClaw may also print a note that the plugin id agentsearch differs from the npm package name. That's expected: agentsearch is the id you use in config.

It lists Status: enabled, Version: 0.1.0 and Capabilities: web-search: agentsearch.

OpenClaw does not auto-select key-free providers, and a plugin can't make itself the default. You pick it once, either from the setup wizard:

and choose AgentSearch, or with one command:

The plugin never changes your search provider by itself, so if you switch back to another provider later, it stays switched.

Because the plugin was installed, restart the gateway so it loads:

The agentsearch entry should show "selected": true:

Then run one search from the command line, without involving a model:

With no wallet, that uses one of your free daily searches. From here, your assistant's web_search tool goes through AgentSearch in any chat.

When the gateway starts, it logs which mode search is in, for example "Search mode: free: AgentSearch free tier, up to 25 searches/day per IP (no wallet configured)". The tool description and every error message state the mode too, so you can always tell whether a search is free or paid.

Free search covers a lot of personal use. If your assistant needs more than 25 searches a day, or the extra filters, add a wallet. There's still no account: the plugin pays each search from a small wallet you control.

Restart the gateway after changing env vars. With a wallet set, the default auto mode pays $0.003 per search and the startup log says "Search mode: paid". Paid mode also accepts the freshness, date-range, country, language and include/exclude-domain filters.

The plugin reads the key only to sign payments. The key never appears in logs, tool output or errors.

Because the cap resets on restart, the wallet balance is your real budget: a looping assistant can't spend more than you put in.

If the same wallet is there for other tools and you want search to stay free, set the mode:

AGENTSEARCH_MODE=free in the gateway environment does the same, and env vars take precedence over plugin config. The modes are auto (the default: paid with a wallet, free without), free and paid.

All plugin settings live under plugins.entries.agentsearch.config.webSearch:

The wallet key itself is env-only: AGENTSEARCH_WALLET_PRIVATE_KEY.

The plugin covers web_search. If you also want your assistant to read full pages as clean markdown, or render JavaScript-heavy sites, add the AgentSearch MCP server through OpenClaw's MCP config:

The probe should report agentsearch: 8 tools. Extract has a free trial of 5 pages a day per IP and render 3 a day per IP; after that they're pay per call over x402 with a wallet.

Failed calls aren't charged. Full details are on the pricing page.

Install cancelled; rerun with --force after reviewing the source. You ran the install without a prompt (in a script, for example). Add --force --accept-capabilities, as in Step 1.

infer web providers doesn't show agentsearch as selected. Run openclaw config set tools.web.search.provider agentsearch again, then check openclaw plugins inspect agentsearch shows Status: enabled.

"AgentSearch free search limit reached". You've used the free searches for your IP today. Wait for the daily reset, or add a wallet as shown above.

You set a wallet but search is still free. Check the gateway log for the mode line. A mode of free in plugin config or AGENTSEARCH_MODE=free in the environment keeps search free. If you set the key in the environment, restart the gateway so it picks it up.

Filters seem to be ignored. Freshness, dates, country, language and domain filters apply in paid mode only.

No. Free search needs nothing at all. Paid search is signed from your own wallet over x402, so there's still no account or key.

Not yet. Install it from npm with openclaw plugins install npm:@agentsearchhq/openclaw-web-search. The source is on GitHub at agentsearchhq-coder/openclaw-agentsearch.

OpenClaw doesn't auto-select key-free search providers, and the plugin never edits your config by itself. Select it once with openclaw configure --section web or openclaw config set tools.web.search.provider agentsearch.

$0.003 per search in USDC on Base, with no daily cap. The plugin never signs more than $0.020 for one search and stops at $1.00 per gateway process by default. Errors and empty results aren't charged.

That's all it takes to give your OpenClaw assistant live web search, free to start. Read more about Search, Extract and Render, or see how the same tools work in Claude Code and a Mastra agent.

Running Multiple Trials per Task for AI Agent Evaluation

📅 Nov 19, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Once an agent completes a task with your product, the next question is how consistently it can do it again. Even with the same instructions, tools, and documentation, it may take a different route, spend longer on the work, or get stuck on something it previously completed easily.

When developers rely on agents to use your product, unpredictable behavior can interrupt their work and make the experience harder to trust. Repeated trials help you find where the workflow breaks down and test whether clearer documentation, better tools, or more useful error messages help them complete tasks more consistently.

Running the same task multiple times helps you measure how consistently an agent succeeds and uncover failures a single run might miss. Each execution, or trial, uses a defined agent configuration and starts independently, with an empty environment and no conversation history or intermediate work carried over from another trial. When comparing product versions, this repetition also helps you assess whether a change improved performance or the results simply varied between runs.

Agent behavior can vary even under apparently deterministic settings. In the 2026 study On Randomness in Agentic Evals, researchers collected 60,000 coding-agent trajectories on SWE-bench Verified. Across ten complete evaluation runs of each configuration, scores varied by 2.2 to 6 percentage points from the lowest to the highest run. Variation persisted even when the temperature of the underlying LLMs was set to zero. For engineers depending on agents to get work done, those inconsistencies can become a recurring source of friction.

Multiple trials also give you more behavior to inspect. A task that passes four times and fails once offers a useful opportunity to compare approaches. Did the agent find different documentation? Choose another tool? Misinterpret a response? Reviewing those differences can point toward changes you can test.

The ranges below offer a starting point for choosing trials per task. You’ll generally need more trials when results change from run to run, you’re looking for a small improvement, or you’re trying to catch a failure that rarely happens.

Suggested Trial Counts by Evaluation Goal

Even major AI labs use different trial counts. OpenAI averaged four attempts per task on SWE-bench Verified, while Anthropic averaged 25 trials. For your evaluation, the useful count depends on how much the results vary and how small an improvement you want to detect. Research on coding agents shows that smaller improvements can require substantially more repetition. Start with a pilot to understand that variability, then use it to plan the trials your comparison needs.

The smaller the improvement you want to detect, the more trials you’ll generally need to distinguish it from normal variation. For example, this coding-agent study estimated that, at its median observed variability, detecting a two-percentage-point improvement would require approximately nine complete benchmark runs per system. Detecting one point would require 36. Those estimates assumed 80% statistical power and a 5% significance threshold, with 500 tasks in each complete run.

There are three important metrics worth understanding when evaluating agents: pass@1, pass@k, and pass^k. The first two grew out of code generation research, where tests could check whether a system produced a working solution in one attempt or among several candidates. OpenAI’s 2021 HumanEval paper refined how to estimate these probabilities.

As evaluations expanded to agents handling everyday workflows, consistency became another important question. The 2024 τ-bench paper introduced pass^k to measure the probability that all k independent trials succeed, averaged across tasks. Together, these metrics describe success on a single attempt, success within multiple attempts, and consistency across repeated runs.

Results from the 2024 τ-bench retail evaluation show why it helps to measure success across repeated trials. GPT-4o’s function-calling agent achieved 61.2% average success on a single attempt, measured by pass@1. Its pass^8 score fell below 25%, meaning that, averaged across tasks, the probability of succeeding in all eight independent trials was less than 25%.

Agent evaluations take time and compute, so choosing between more tasks and more trials comes down to what you need to learn. Add tasks when important workflows or conditions are missing. Run more trials when you need stronger evidence about how consistently agents handle the cases you already cover.

Choosing between more tasks and more trials

Repeated trials help you understand how consistently an agent handles a task, but the benefit of repetition has limits. Once you have a clearer picture of that behavior, testing more representative tasks, rather than more trials of a smaller set of tasks, may tell you more about performance across your product. Use your initial results to decide where uncertainty remains. If you’re trying to detect a small improvement, you may need more tasks, more trials, or both.

Use repeated trials to assess how reliably agents complete the workflows your product supports. Inspect outcomes and traces, identify where behavior varies, and test whether changes to tools, documentation, or error messages address those problems. Balance repetition with representative task coverage so your results support conclusions about both individual workflows and the broader product experience.

Apple’s head of camera and Photos software has retired, per report

📅 Nov 19, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

According to a new report from Bloomberg, Apple’s head of camera and photo software retired last month. Jon McCormack spent eight years at Apple and “oversaw the Camera and Photos apps and the underlying technology that determines how users capture, view, and edit their pictures and video.”

Mark Gurman reports that Apple hasn’t named a new vice president to replace McCormack, who reported to software chief Craig Federighi. “Instead, his former deputies are assuming his responsibilities under other executives,” the report says.

In his role, McCormack oversaw “close coordination between software engineers and Apple’s camera hardware and chip teams.” He’s credited with last year’s redesigned selfie camera and other projects:

His work included the redesigned selfie camera introduced with the iPhone 17 lineup last year. This year, Apple added a variable-aperture camera to the iPhone 18 Pro lineup, giving users more control over lighting and depth of field. The company also introduced a customizable Camera app interface with professional controls.

Bloomberg describes McCormack as a “strong opponent of using generative AI to create or alter photographs,” a philosophy that created tension as Apple embraced AI tools in the Photos app. His retirement, however, “wasn’t related to a disagreement or clash with Apple over its AI strategy,” the report says.

McCormack spoke in depth about iOS 27 features like Clean Up, Extend, and Spatial Reframing in an interview with Tyler Stalman in June. He emphasized how the features “preserve the integrity of the moment.”

Fresha Becomes a Growth Engine for Beauty and Wellness Businesses, Connecting Partners With Customers Across the World's Leading Search and AI Platforms

📅 Nov 19, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

LONDON, Nov. 19, 2026 /PRNewswire/ -- Fresha, the world's leading beauty and wellness booking platform, has announced the launch of the Fresha Partner Network, transforming its global Marketplace into a powerful growth engine that connects beauty and wellness businesses with customers across the world's leading search engines, AI assistants and digital platforms. Bringing together deeper and more advanced integrations across  Google Gemini and AI Mode, Google Search, Google Maps, ChatGPT, Claude, and other AI platforms and with Alexa joining in early 2027, the network enables Fresha partners to reach potential clients at the exact moment they're searching for services, turning online discovery into new customer relationships and confirmed appointments.

Building on the Fresha Marketplace, which facilitates more than 35 million appointments every month, the Partner Network extends Fresha's reach beyond its own app and website into the digital platforms where consumers increasingly begin their booking journeys. Whether someone is searching Google for a last-minute haircut, asking ChatGPT to recommend a nearby facial or exploring beauty services through an AI assistant, Fresha connects relevant, bookable businesses directly with that demand. Every Fresha partner is included automatically through their existing Fresha Marketplace profile, making their service menus, pricing, customer reviews, real-time appointment availability, professional profiles, service portfolios, images and other rich media accessible across supported platforms, without requiring additional setup or the management of separate integrations.

The commercial opportunity behind AI-powered discovery is already evident in Fresha's performance. Between January and October 2026, consumer traffic referred to Fresha through AI assistants increased by more than 640%, with ChatGPT accounting for the overwhelming majority of referrals. Fresha's daily impressions across Google's AI Overviews also more than doubled between early September and the end of October 2026. As AI-powered search becomes an increasingly important channel for customer discovery, the Fresha Partner Network positions beauty and wellness businesses to capitalize on this growing demand, connecting consumer searches with relevant services and real-time appointment availability.

A major component of the launch is Fresha's expanded integration with Google, bringing live appointment availability directly into Google Search, Google Maps and Google's AI-powered search experiences. Rather than simply directing consumers to an external booking page, Fresha provides real-time availability, allowing potential clients to explore available appointments and book directly from their search experience. By simplifying the journey from discovery to confirmation, Fresha helps businesses capture high-intent consumer demand, turning online visibility into confirmed appointments.

The network also builds on Fresha's pioneering integrations with ChatGPT and Claude, which have been live since August 2026. Between March and October 2026, ChatGPT-referred sessions increased more than sixfold to over 151,000 per month, while sessions containing a booking event grew 30-fold, highlighting the growing role of AI-powered discovery in driving booking activity. As the first beauty and wellness booking platform to introduce native integrations with both AI assistants, Fresha enables consumers to discover relevant businesses through natural-language conversations, with personalized recommendations informed by location, services, pricing, reviews and live availability. From finding a highly rated barbershop nearby to searching for a massage within a particular budget, these integrations connect consumer intent with real businesses and bookable appointments. Alexa's planned addition in early 2027 will further extend the network into voice-powered discovery.

For enterprise businesses, franchises and multi-location groups, the Fresha Partner Network provides a scalable customer acquisition channel across entire portfolios. As operators expand into new cities, enter international markets or open additional locations, each business's existing Fresha Marketplace profile automatically connects them to an expanding ecosystem of search and AI platforms. With services, pricing, reviews and availability presented consistently across supported channels, businesses can strengthen their digital presence, attract customers across multiple markets and manage appointments through Fresha's centralized platform, without additional operational complexity.

All Partner Network bookings are consolidated within Fresha Marketplace reporting, giving businesses a centralized view of appointments generated through the network. For enterprise groups, franchises and multi-location operators, this provides greater visibility into customer acquisition performance across locations and markets, helping teams monitor booking activity, identify emerging opportunities and make more informed decisions about business growth.

"The way consumers discover and book beauty and wellness services is fundamentally changing, and we want every Fresha partner to benefit from that shift," said Michaela Peicheva, Senior Product Manager at Fresha. "We're transforming Fresha into a global growth engine, connecting businesses with customers at the exact moment they're searching for a service. By extending our Marketplace across Google, ChatGPT, Claude and other leading platforms, we're making it easier for businesses of every size to attract new clients, fill their calendars and grow, without adding complexity to how they operate."

The launch of the Fresha Partner Network marks a significant expansion of Fresha's global Marketplace, establishing Fresha as the central hub connecting beauty and wellness businesses with the next generation of AI-powered discovery. As AI agents become increasingly embedded in how consumers search for and book services, Fresha's ambition is to become the go-to platform for AI assistants seeking trusted, real-time beauty and wellness information. By bringing together verified business profiles, services, pricing, reviews, rich media and live appointment availability within one connected ecosystem, Fresha is building a trusted, all-in-one infrastructure that powers intelligent discovery and booking across the world's leading digital platforms. With further integrations planned, the Partner Network positions Fresha at the center of an evolving AI ecosystem, connecting businesses with new audiences and turning consumer intent into confirmed appointments and long-term growth.

Fresha is the world's leading AI-powered booking platform for beauty and wellness, trusted by over 140,000 partner businesses across more than 120 countries. Its fully integrated ecosystem spans scheduling, payments, marketing, team management and a global consumer marketplace, giving beauty and wellness businesses everything they need to operate, grow and scale. The Fresha Marketplace connects millions of consumers with salons, spas, barbershops and wellness studios worldwide, with over one million monthly downloads and more than $1.4 billion in monthly transaction value. To learn more, visit fresha.com.

📅 Nov 19, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

America’s favorite quiz show is welcoming fans to Washington, D.C., for a three-day event celebrating all things “Jeopardy!” Taking place May 7 to 9, 2027, the convention will feature live programming, engaging panels, interactive quizzes and exclusive merchandise.

Jeopardy!Con will feature behind-the-scenes sessions with iconic players from the trivia show, plus exclusive announcements and contestant casting opportunities. The weekend will also include a welcome party, closing celebration and other social gatherings. Related Stories Queen Latifah, Bone Thugs-N-Harmony, MC Lyte and More to Lead 2027 Rock the Bells Cruise

Perhaps the most exciting aspect of Jeopardy!Con is an all-new live “Jeopardy!” competition format featuring famous players competing in front of thousands of fans. “The two-night marquee event will bring the game to life on a scale never before seen at ‘Jeopardy!,'” according to a press release.

Tickets will go on sale in early 2027, with additional programming to be announced.

“People are looking for meaningful ways to connect, and few things bring people together quite like a shared passion,” said Suzanne Prete, president of game shows at Sony Pictures Television. “For generations, ‘Jeopardy!’ has united fans through a love of knowledge, curiosity and competition. Jeopardy!Con builds on that legacy in an entirely new way, creating an unprecedented opportunity to bring fans together to celebrate the show, connect with one another and experience the franchise like never before.”

“Ever since I joined ‘Jeopardy!’ in 2021, I’ve wanted to bring this extraordinary community together in a way that’s bigger than anything we’ve done before and give fans the chance to interact with their quiz show heroes,” said Michael Davies, executive producer of “Jeopardy!” “One of the remarkable things we’ve seen through our live podcast and ‘Jeopardy!’ interactive events all over the country is just how much people want to come together and share their love of the game. Everyone on the team is incredibly excited to bring it all together next May.”
